In 2021, Applegate revealed she had been diagnosed with MS. She retired from acting in 2022 following the finale of the Netflix series Dead to Me. Since then, the 54-year-old actress has advocated for the MS community and launched the MeSsY podcast with Sopranos star Sigler, who was diagnosed with MS in 2001.

I don’t know what’s going on,” Applegate said. “This is what I’m still living with, and I never had that before. I was able to sit up and stuff.”

She added that she recently lost “too much” weight and said, “I don’t know what’s going on.”

“The more you lose in your face, the older you look. Like, the jowls are more pronounced now than they were before because it’s all hollow and I just look older,” she explained.

#3679. Will King Charles Be the Last Windsor to Lead the Commonwealth?

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

According to a report by The Times, King Charles is reportedly aware that he may be the last member of the British royal family to serve as head of the Commonwealth. The reason? His son William, the future monarch of England, reportedly has no intention of succeeding him in this role as well. A prospect that, if confirmed, would mark a significant change in relations between the British monarchy and the organization that brings together 56 countries. For while it is true that William is destined to inherit the throne, it is by no means certain that he wishes to retain every responsibility and tradition associated with the Crown. (Vanity Fair has reached out to Buckingham Palace for comment on the Times report.)

The issue, in reality, is more complex than it might seem. Unlike the British throne, in fact, the leadership of the Commonwealth is not automatically passed from a sovereign to their heir. It is a symbolic role, and succession is decided by the leaders of the member states. Charles assumed the role after the death of his mother, Elizabeth, but his appointment had been agreed upon by the Commonwealth heads of government in 2018. William, therefore, would not be obligated to accept it.

According to The Times’ report, the Prince of Wales has indicated that he does not wish to become head of the organization when he ascends to the throne. However, this is neither an official announcement from Kensington Palace nor a decision that has already been formalized. To understand the significance of this potential decision, we must look back to Elizabeth. During her 70-year reign, the monarch made the Commonwealth one of the pillars of her institutional work, guiding the transformation of an organization tied to the history of the British Empire into a voluntary association of independent states.

Charles has embraced that legacy with conviction. His commitment to the Commonwealth is an important part of his diplomatic vision and the relationship he intends to maintain with countries united by a shared—and often complex—history. And it is precisely this complexity that makes the current moment particularly delicate. The king is, in fact, preparing for a trip to the Caribbean, scheduled for October 27 through November 4, that will include stops in the Bahamas, Guyana, and Antigua and Barbuda.

This visit comes as the debate over Britain’s colonial past, slavery, and calls for economic reparations continues to take center stage in relations between London and several Caribbean nations.

For some time now, however, Prince William has been associated with the idea of a more streamlined monarchy, with a smaller number of members engaged in official duties and a greater focus on the causes he considers priorities, from the environment to mental health. In this context, a potential relinquishment of the leadership of the Commonwealth could be interpreted as a further sign of change. This does not necessarily mean distancing himself from member countries, but rather a different interpretation of the role the British monarch should play in relation to them.

After all, the Commonwealth does not consist solely of territories that recognize Charles as head of state. It also includes numerous republics, and his leadership does not entail governing powers over member countries. William’s potential selection would therefore raise a primarily symbolic question: Who could represent the organization after Charles, and what relationship would they wish to maintain with the monarchy?

For Charles, who is deeply attached to the Crown’s international role and to continuity with his mother’s reign, the prospect of being the last Windsor to lead the Commonwealth would hold historical significance. For William, on the other hand, it could represent a way to distinguish the constitutional responsibilities of the future sovereign from those belonging to a particular phase of British history.

For now, this is merely a scenario, not a succession that has already been decided. But the question raised by The Times goes beyond an institutional role: It concerns how the British monarchy intends to redefine its presence in the world. The future King William will certainly inherit a crown. But it may be precisely what he chooses not to inherit that best reflects the monarchy to come.

Originally published in Vanity Fair Italy.

#5251. STAT+: After two deaths in autoimmune cell therapy trial, Artiva is shutting down

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Test your knowledge with our new weekday mini crossword

Elaine’s stories explore the boom in new obesity treatments, new drug payment models, the ways companies are affected by FDA changes, and the emerging psychedelics drug sector. She co-writes The Readout newsletter, and co-hosts STAT’s weekly biotech podcast, The Readout Loud. You can reach Elaine on Signal at elaineywchen.70.

Artiva Biotherapeutics said Tuesday that it’s winding down after two patients died in a trial testing its cell therapy for autoimmune diseases, the latest in a series of safety events that have emerged in a promising research field.

One participant in the Phase 2 study died from a neurological complication after receiving the treatment, an NK cell therapy. Researchers determined the death was likely not related to the cell therapy itself, but to a chemotherapy that was given before the cell therapy, Artiva said. It’s standard to give patients chemotherapies before infusing cell therapies to lower the patients’ white blood cell count and make room for the new cells.

A second participant experienced sudden death about two months after treatment. Researchers determined that this was not related to any component of the treatment process, but the cause of death has not been determined, the San Diego-based company said.

#3698. The Queen That Never Was: The Twisted Tale of Lady Arbella Stuart

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Early in her reign, Queen Elizabeth I—sensing her court was teeming with doubters and double-crossers—made it clear she’d cede her power to no husband. This also meant, of course, she would not produce an heir, which must have turned her bloodline-crazed dad, Henry VIII, in his tomb.

Elizabeth I, Queen of England and Ireland, 1588. Version of the Armada portrait attributed to George Gower.

What’s more, she refused to name a successor. “Assuredly, if my successor were known to the world, I would never esteem my state to be safe,” she told parliament in 1559. Into this vacuum stepped an array of noble sons and daughters with even the flimsiest claim to the throne. “In the 1590s, there were at times as many as a dozen viable contenders to succeed Elizabeth I,” said Elizabeth Goldring, art historian and author of Nicholas Hilliard: Life of an Artist.

In 2024 Goldring, along with Emma Rutherford, who runs The Limner Company, a portrait miniature consultancy, discovered a long lost miniature of one such claimant to the throne: 16-year-old Lady Arbella Stuart, great-great-granddaughter of Henry VII, the first Tudor king, and niece of the doomed Mary Queen of Scots. Described by her first cousin (twice removed) Elizabeth I as “an eaglet of her own kind,” and by the Venetian ambassador as having “very exalted ideas,” Arbella was at times favored above the Queen’s eventual Scottish successor, James I. “Arbella is one of the greatest ‘might haves’ of history,” said Rutherford.

The rediscovery of this long-forgotten portrait, which sold earlier this year, pushes Arbella’s almost-unbelievable story back to the forefront of Tudor and Elizabethan history, an age littered with tales of highborn daughters moving about like pawns on a chessboard, but few got as close to the throne as Arbella.

Elizabeth Hardwick, Countess of Shrewsbury, “Bess of Hardwick,” 1520–1608.

Raised in gilded captivity by her dynastically inclined grandmother, Bess of Hardwick, who described Arbella as her “jewel” (to be prized, polished and hidden away), Arbella grew up isolated and believing she’d be queen, much like Queen Victoria centuries later. Unlike Victoria, that never happened. Such exalted notions can’t be good for the teenage psyche (she would suffer from fragile, and at times dangerously unstable, mental health for the rest of her life.)

In the spring of 2023, Rutherford was contacted by the then-owner of the miniature to see if she could identify the sitter. She brought in Goldring, who had written a book on the miniature’s painter, Nicholas Hilliard. “Emma and I ended up researching the miniature together for the next twelve months and having a whale of a time on what turned out to be an incredible journey of discovery,” said Goldring.

There were hints. “After almost thirty years of looking at portrait miniatures it takes a lot to make my heart beat faster,” said Rutherford. “I had never seen anything like it…there were so many clues in the painting which could help us identify the sitter.”

Bess of Hardwick was a meticulous keeper of household expenses, so Rutherford and Goldring were able to trace a payment to England’s premier portrait miniaturist of the time, Hilliard, as well as tips paid to Elizabeth I’s gardeners at Greenwich Palace, where the portrait was painted. They were also aware Hilliard had painted Arbella in the early 1590s, “albeit the conventional wisdom was that the resulting portrait was no longer extant,” said Goldring. “It seemed almost too good to be true that we could have found Hilliard’s portrait of her...But the deeper we dug in the archives, the more evidence we found relating to this commission, all of which pointed to the sitter being Arbella.”

Forgotten for centuries and likely tucked away at the back of a drawer or cupboard, the watercolor portrait was “remarkably fresh and vibrant”, said Goldring. As Rutherford recalls, “it was unglazed and unframed and loosely held in a Victorian leather case. One sneeze could have obliterated something that had survived for over 400 years virtually untouched.”

Queen Elizabeth I, “The Ditchley portrait” by Marcus Gheeraerts, 1592.

Though small in size, the portrait tells a story of soaring ambitions. In the summer of 1592, Grandma Bess hired Hilliard to paint Arbella standing in the flowering gardens of Greenwich Palace dressed in the impossibly-high-ruffed dress of a queen, and making what Goldring realized was a preposterously bold allusion to the famous “Ditchley portrait” of Elizabeth I from the same year (now hanging in the National Portrait Gallery in London).

“This was a miniature designed to present Arbella as, among other things, a Queen-in-waiting, another Elizabeth I, but younger and still of child-bearing age,” said Goldring. “Note all the images of fertility and fruitfulness in that garden.”

Small and therefore easy to transport, portrait miniatures such as these were designed to be used in dynastic marriage negotiations between Europe’s royal courts and powerful families. But as Goldring and Rutherword would discover, this particular miniature was more than just a pretty picture of a would-be bride.

“One of the most extraordinary things Emma and I discovered was that Hilliard’s miniature of Arbella was, at one stage, intended for use by the Elizabethan spy services as a prop in a plot to wrong-foot Philip II of Spain,” said Goldring. By leading Philip into believing Arbella was marrying the son of his ally, the Duke of Parma, Protestant England could drive a wedge between two Continental Catholic powers.

“Arbella stands with the world at her feet: She is presented as royal, she is poised to marry into an alliance which will be for England’s good, but she is about to lose it all,” said Rutherford. In the end the marriage plot fell through and the portrait never made it to the continent. Tucked away and forgotten for centuries, it served as a woebegotten metaphor for Arbella’s life.

“When she failed to fulfill her one role in life to become queen, and then her grandmother died a few years later, she rebelled on a grand scale,” said Rutherford. In her 30s Arbella made a last ditch grab for power—or simply agency—when she married William Seymour (another former claimant to the throne) in secret. Word of the marriage reached the top, as it always does (Will these courtiers never learn?) and Arbella’s former rival for the crown, now King James I, had the couple locked up, separately.

Arbella escaped, disguised as a man in a wig, boots and sword, with plans to flee to France with her ill-begotten husband. Seymour made it to safety and prospered like a man. She was caught and sent to the Tower. In a tragically poetic twist of fate, she died in 1615 following a hunger strike in what were likely the former Tower lodgings of her forebear, Anne Boleyn, that other dynastic pawn-turned-power player of the Tudor age.

“I think that this portrait has gone a long way to reviving an interest in Arbella. It has humanized someone who was previously just a footnote in history and fleshed out her story in a way that perhaps no other artefact might have done,” said Rutherford. “And everyone loves a new discovery.”

#410. Trump-appointed CDC deputy director to resign after six months on the job

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Jennifer Shuford is expected to leave her post after midterm elections ‘to spend more time with my family’

The deputy director and chief medical officer of the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC) will resign after six months on the job, according to an agency email obtained by the Guardian.

Dr Jennifer Shuford was appointed by Donald Trump in April as part of a four-person senior leadership team.

The appointments were meant to stabilize the agency after the previous CDC director, Susan Monarez, left less than a month into the job – publicly describing the directives of the health secretary, Robert F Kennedy Jr, as “unscientific”.

“I am leaving to spend more time with my family,” Shuford said in a statement, sent via email by the CDC director, Dr Erica Schwartz. “I am so thankful to President Trump and Secretary Kennedy for giving me the opportunity to help lead CDC. It has been an amazing experience, and I will be forever grateful for it.”

Shuford is expected to leave her post on 12 November, after the midterm elections.

The deputy director was appointed as part of a four-person team in April, alongside Schwartz and two other top leaders. Shuford’s appointment was cheered by public health leaders, such as Association of State and Territorial Health Officials (ASTHO), who would have known her work as Texas health commissioner.

Trump described the team as “Highly Respected Doctors of Medicine [who] have the knowledge, experience, and TOP degrees to restore the GOLD STANDARD OF SCIENCE at the CDC”, in a social media post.

In the time since Shuford has worked at the agency, Kennedy has made significant changes that undermine vaccine trust and access. In just a few recent examples: Kennedy fired all 17 of the CDC’s expert vaccine advisory panel members and replaced them with ideologically aligned appointees, slow-walked death updates to the agency’s measles dashboard, and forced the CDC to publicly contradict scientific consensus on vaccines and autism – a change the Autism Science Foundation said left it “appalled”.

At the same time, the agency has lost one-third of its workforce and 48 distinct measles outbreaks have roiled the US, particularly in Pennsylvania. The overwhelming majority of Americans – including Maha supporters – trust vaccines.

“I am deeply grateful for Dr Shuford’s outstanding leadership and commitment to CDC and public health, especially as she has navigated an exceptionally demanding, high-profile, and constantly evolving environment over the past several months,” said Schwartz in the email announcing Shuford’s departure.

Dr Satish Pillai will serve as acting deputy director and chief medical officer as the team searches for a successor to Shuford.

#5291. <a href="https://www.fiercehealthcare.com/payers/alignment-healthcare-sues-cms-over-its-2027-medicare-advantage-star-ratings" hreflang="en">Aetna sues to challenge 2027 MA star ratings</a>

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →
Aetna sues to challenge 2027 MA star ratings" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

CVS Heath's Aetna is joining the ranks of insurers suing over its results in the 2027 Medicare Advantage star ratings.

The insured filed suit Monday in Georgia's Southern District Court, and argues that 28 measures used by the Centers for Medicare & Medicaid Services to calculate its scores were "unlawfully adopted" and should have been excluded.

The court is the same venue that ruled in favor of Clover Health over the summer, and set aside 20 measures that were used to determine its 2026 scores. That decision, which the feds have appealed, kicked off a broad recalculation of 2026 scores that did look different for insurers outside of Clover, triggering a wave of litigation.

In its lawsuit, Aetna cites that Clover decision in finding that CMS extended beyond its statutory authority in deploying those 20 measures.

The company said that calculating its scores using the 28 measures in question will likely cost it $2 billion in 2028 quality bonus payments and rebate retention. Those are funds that the insurer could "apply toward expanding member benefits, reducing premiums and investing in quality improvement."

Aetna adds that in addition to the monetary loss, having lower scores negatively impacts the company's reputation and its ability to attract potential members.

"The harm to CVS Health is not exclusively financial; lower ratings carry meaningful reputational harm, which can in turn impact membership," per the lawsuit. "By incorporating unlawful measures into the 2027 Star Ratings calculation, CMS cast CVS Health’s ratings in a poorer light in the eyes of Medicare Advantage consumers relative to peer MAOs, undermining CVS Health’s ability to compete for Medicare beneficiaries through enhanced benefits and the like."

Aetna did not respond to a request for comment at the time of publication.

Based on an analysis from Stephens, 69% of Aetna's MA members are in plans with four or more stars for 2027, down from 81%. That threshold is the key to securing quality bonus payments.

Aetna's complaint follows others focused on the 2027 scores from Centene and Alignment Healthcare (see those stories below).

Centene has become the latest insurer to sue the federal government over its 2027 Medicare Advantage star ratings.

In a lawsuit filed Thursday in Georgia's Southern District Court, the company argues that the Centers for Medicare & Medicaid Services violated the Administrative Procedure Act in determining its latest scores. Centene says that CMS ran afoul of "core statutory safeguards" in deploying 17 measures.

The use of those measures ultimately caused "significant injury" to the company, according to the complaint. For 2027, 9% of Centene's members are enrolled in plans with at least four stars, which declined from 18% in the prior year.

"In implementing each of the individual Star measures that Centene challenges, CMS violated applicable law and thereby caused Centene to lose out on hundreds of millions of dollars in revenue that Centene could use to strengthen benefits, reduce premiums, and improve health outcomes," the company said in the lawsuit.

CMS argues that the court has already sided with a similar argument in its decision in favor of Clover Health earlier this year. In that case, which led to a broad recalculation in scores, the courts set aside 20 measures that were used in determining Clover's star ratings for 2026.

If the 17 measures in question had been excluded in calculating Centene's scores, the company estimates it would receive $448 million more in quality bonus payments for the 2028 plan year, funds it would deploy to expand benefits, lower premiums and enhance programs like care coordination.

In addition to the financial impact, a drop in star ratings also carries a reputational hit, Centene said.

"Beyond the financial impact to the company and its members, depressed ratings also inflict significant reputational damage and diminished membership," the company said in the complaint

Centene had not returned a request for comment at the time of publication.

Alignment Healthcare sued federal regulators, alleging that its 2027 Medicare Advantage star ratings were determined "in flagrant violation" of statute.

In the lawsuit filed Friday in District of Columbia federal court, the insurer says that the Centers for Medicare & Medicaid Services "has completely departed from Congress’s vision of leveraging predictable, valid measures developed with input from providers, plans, and the public" in establishing the methodology for the star ratings.

The company argues that rather than establish "consistent, stable specifications" for metrics through the traditional rule-making process, the agency instead detailed key measures through a series of "technical notes." This violates the Administrative Procedure Act, per the lawsuit.

The program's star ratings are the critical determinant in quality bonus payments to insurers as well as a tool that aims to make it easier for enrollees and brokers to identify benefits with the highest quality.

"Consistent application of the statutory requirements governing Star Ratings is therefore essential not only to plan payments and competition but also to ensuring that beneficiaries can rely on the Star Ratings as meaningful and comparable measures of plan quality," Alignment said in the lawsuit.

The company adds that multiple recent court decisions have set aside CMS' star ratings methodology, notably a ruling over the summer in favor of Clover Health that tossed 20 metrics used in the 2026 calculations. A Georgia federal judge determined that 10 measures utilized exceeded CMS' authority, while 10 other measures were promulgated inappropriately.

CMS recalculated the scores across the board following the ruling, but only set aside the 10 measures that the courts determined were outside the bounds of its regulatory authority. The agency maintained that approach in the 2027 calculations, Alignment argues.

"Congress created the Star Ratings program to allow Medicare Advantage beneficiaries to make informed decisions about plan enrollment based on clinically validated measures of plan quality that had been subjected to notice and comment," the company said. "CMS’s contrary approach fails to carry out the vision that Congress set forth by statute."

The Trump administration has appealed the Clover decision, so that legal case remains ongoing.

In addition, Alignment argues it suffered reputational and financial damage as a result of its 2027 scores, which plummeted compared to the prior year. Just 25% of its members are in plans with four or more stars for 2027, down from 100% in 2026.

Shares in the company tumbled following the release of the ratings, though stock prices have rebounded since.

Alignment sued the feds following the Clover decision to secure a recalculation for its scores that aligns with the court's decision, and in a statement to Fierce Healthcare, said that the lawsuit aligns with that perspective.

"Our lawsuit seeks to ensure that the Star Ratings program operates as Congress intended and that Alignment is properly compensated for the care we provide," Alignment said in the statement. "We respect the judicial process and will present our case in court. Because this matter involves pending litigation, we will not comment on the specific legal arguments beyond our filings."

"Our focus remains on delivering exceptional care, improving health outcomes and advocating for a stable, lawful regulatory framework that supports seniors and strengthens the Medicare Advantage program," the company said.

A few years ago, parents rushed their baby to a small rural hospital. But the facility didn’t have pediatric specialists, pediatric nurses, or a children’s intensive care unit. As the little boy’s condition worsened, the hospital’s staff scrambled to identify a facility that could take him. But every children’s hospital in northern California was overwhelmed. No one had room for one more.

The baby died of RSV. And the problem has only gotten worse since then, write two pediatric care experts in a new First Opinion.

“In this new normal, community hospitals across the country are transferring children to dedicated pediatric centers not just because a child is critically ill, but increasingly because smaller hospitals have quietly lost the ability to manage even common conditions,” they write. “The nation’s relatively few children’s hospitals can’t accommodate every sick child, much as we would like to. We now routinely turn sick children away and hope one of our colleagues somewhere else will have a bed.”

Brain-eating amoebas are the stuff of zombie novels and spooky season. They are also, unfortunately, real-world parasites found in warm freshwater. Infections caused by N. Fowleri are hard to treat and nearly always fatal. But researchers have isolated a compound that could help stop the disease.

The substance was taken from a marine bacterium, and has been able to kill multiple strains of N. Fowleri faster than the current treatment. There is much work to be done before this discovery can translate to medicine though. Researchers say their next focus will be studying whether the compound can cross the blood-brain barrier.

#4584. This Is Your Brain on Weed. It’s Actually Kind of Amazing.

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

In November 1996, California became the first state to legalize medical cannabis. (Many) politics and (some) physicians promised us breakthroughs in better health. Thirty years later, how are we really doing? Men’s Health decided to find out in this special report package. Read more here.

WHEN YOU USE weed, your brain kind of goes all over the place. You likely know about how marijuana can chill you out, make you forget yourself, and induce the munchies. But there are other fascinating ways that the drug can alter your mind.

When THC binds to receptors in your hippocampus, the compound disrupts signals and impairs memory. That’s why you may forget what you were saying mid-sentence or where you put your keys when stoned.

THC also binds to receptors in the amygdala, which can help you feel chill when high—and paranoid or anxious when you’re too high.

The back of the brain, the cerebellum, is important for balance, motor skills, and time perception. When THC gets involved, you might feel clumsier and as if time is slowing.

THC disrupts the prefrontal cortex, which oversees critical thinking, problem-solving, and processing information from your senses. As a result, you’re going to find it harder to multitask, feel more impulsive, and zone out.

THC also stimulates the release of feel-good dopamine in the nucleus accumbens. If you’re a chronic, heavy user, cannabis can dull dopamine response and potentially rewire circuitry within the nucleus accumbens, making you more susceptible to addiction.

Your prefrontal cortex and limbic system contribute to imaginative ideas. THC can bind to receptors in these regions and may reduce self-critical noise, allowing for greater free expression.

Scientists believe that the anterior cingulate cortex is central to the perception of pain, and THC seems to dampen its ability—in a good way.

There’s so much more going on in the world of weed and health. Read more here.

#4594. Weed Addiction Is a Real Thing. The World's Leading Cannabis Researcher Explains Why.

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

In November 1996, California became the first state to legalize medical cannabis. (Many) politics and (some) physicians promised us breakthroughs in better health. Thirty years later, how are we really doing? Men’s Health decided to find out in this special report package. Read more here.

KEVIN HILL, MD, the guy when it comes to this stuff. He’s the chief of the Division of Addiction Psychiatry at Beth Israel Deaconess Medical Center. We talked to him about all the questions you probably have about weed and addiction.

Dr. Hill: Absolutely. Most people who use cannabis don’t develop a cannabis use disorder or cannabis addiction. But there has always been a subset of individuals who do. Five years ago, the data indicated about 10 to 30 percent of adults develop a cannabis use disorder. Now the published data says 30 to 35 percent. With the rise in potency, we’re seeing a rise in the number of users who are developing problems.

MH: Does cannabis use disorder differ from other addictions?

Dr. Hill: It affects a lot of the same neurotransmitters that other substances of abuse might affect. If you use cannabis, you’re going to have a surge in dopamine. Similarly, if you use opioids, you’re going to have a surge in dopamine, but the scale of it is different. It’s why a lot of medications we use to treat addictions operate on common pathways.

MH: Is there a genetic risk of developing a weed addiction?

Dr. Hill: Yes. The genetic aspects of cannabis use disorder are probably less well developed than for other addictions, but if people have a family history of addiction of any kind, then it makes them more likely to have another substance use disorder, which could be cannabis.

There’s so much more going on in the world of weed and health. Read more here.

#4603. 30 Years of Legal Weed: Has It Made Us Any Healthier?

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Our product picks are editor-tested, expert-approved. We may earn a commission through links on our site. Why Trust Us?

The very highs and bizarre lows of America on medicinal marijuana.

EARLIER THIS YEAR, during a brief and mercifully abandoned period when I was considering doing stand-up as a hobby, I went to a comedy show in downtown Austin. I was legitimately shocked to find, as part of the two-drink minimum bill of fare, the option for several different flavors of $5 THC seltzers. This wasn’t “take a pull off your vape or smoke a roach in the alley before the show” territory. No, marijuana drinks were on the menu.

In the past few years, my only social activities have been poker, competitive trivia, and going to the movies, all activities people do stoned, but not activities where you can literally order drugs from the bar alongside your chicken wings. The comedy club, on the other hand, considered marijuana products as a profit center.

This was one of those lightly attended weeknight shows where the comedians banter with the audience because they haven’t prepared much material. So, during a Q&A, I brought up the fact that there were THC sodas available to order, in laminate. “Sir, this is 2026,” the host said.

This is where we are now, a full three decades after California legalized medical weed. But is that good for us?

As I write this, it’s been eight-and-a-half years since I quit marijuana, a drug that at one point made pretty much anything—listening to music, shopping for groceries, cleaning the house—fun for me.

But really, I’ve been sober since I stopped using THC, since I was hardly just smoking there at the end. I was vaping, eating, drinking, dabbing, and otherwise gorging myself on what has become an extremely potent, psychosis-inducing drug. And I wasn’t having fun.

My breaking point, which I recounted in my 2020 memoir Pothead, came at Game 2 of the 2017 World Series at Dodger Stadium, where I was so wasted on weed that I had a screaming meltdown in front of security when I found out the ticket I’d bought was fraudulent.

It was a low point, a bottom so deep and so pathetic that I should have quit right there. But I went on for another three weeks before I finally crawled to a Marijuana Anonymous meeting and asked for help.

Now, nearly a decade later, I’ve emerged from my cocoon to find that THC is everywhere. I live in a state where it’s still technically illegal, but you can find some sort of THC variant in most convenience stores and in most bars and nightclubs.

My friend is an attorney who represents the Texas Alcoholic Beverage Commission. He says his client is in a near panic. Sales are down for every beverage except for nonalcoholic mixers and those THC seltzers. THC is the beer, wine, and vodka of the present day, combined.

And so we find ourselves in a bizarre situation where people are drinking way less alcohol because it’s unhealthy for you, but they are instead ingesting THC products—either through soda, gummies, or any number of other concoctions—from unknown manufacturers, with unknown formulas and malleable dosages.

They’re also vaping, which emerging research is showing is kind of awful for our lungs. A University of Michigan study recently showed that teens who vaped cannabis were twice as likely to experience respiratory symptoms like “wheezing or whistling” in the chest than those who vaped nicotine or smoked traditional cigarettes.

Unlike traditional marijuana, this stuff isn’t “from the earth.” Who knows where it’s really from—or where it’s going?

When I quit weed in 2017, and somewhat loudly and publicly announced myself as an addict, I got the requisite amount of sympathy, but also a tremendous amount of pushback. Marijuana isn’t dangerous, people said. It isn’t addictive. It’s actually good for you.

In 2016, Gavin Newsom, then California’s lieutenant governor, spearheaded the legalization push for recreational marijuana. Cannabis sales have produced more than $8 billion in tax revenue for California since retail sales began in 2018, with a decent proportion of that funneled into teen substance-abuse prevention programs. But the state has also seen an increase in children suffering from cannabis poisoning.

It’s been a massive shift, moving from medically administered weed for veterans and cancer patients to powerful THC beverages you can buy at your bodega.

Legalization has created a new frontier of intoxicants without a centralized warning system for users. Data from a 2025 national study of U.S. adults stated that 15 percent of respondents reported daily or near-daily cannabis use.

Among those daily users, 18 percent reported experiencing symptoms consistent with cannabinoid hyperemesis syndrome, a disorder characterized by cyclic, severe nausea, intractable vomiting, and gut pain alleviated temporarily by hot baths or showers.

This translates to an estimated 7.2 million people in the United States alone, many of them teenagers, experiencing these symptoms. That number is probably vastly underestimated, given that fewer than 12 percent of sufferers have received a formal diagnosis.

Welcome to the darker side of legalized weed, a world of cannabis-induced psychosis, “scromiting” (yes, screaming while vomiting), and concoctions like “shatter” or “wax,” which contain up to 90 percent THC, as opposed to the 3 to 5 percent in joints from previous decades.

So I find myself in a somewhat odd situation. I was a relatively early victim of modern weed’s potency, as well as a relatively early unadopter, and a very early voice in the wilderness warning the world that marijuana might not be the answer to all our health concerns. But now I find myself, like a patient waking up from a long coma, in a world where THC is as prevalent as, and cheaper than, whiskey. Yet still, I find myself thinking it should be legal. So which is it? Maybe we still don’t have enough data.

Thirty years since the start of legalization might feel like a long time, but it’s really not. In terms of scientific analysis, we’re still in relatively undiscovered country. If you’re suffering from chronic pain, or dying of cancer, marijuana is a healthier option than, say, oxycodone. But for the rest of us? No one really knows.

The only thing I can be truly sure of is that it’s no longer for me.

‘Scromiting’ and Other Super Scary Symptoms of Taking High-THC Weed

#4628. This Is What Weed Really Did to My Body

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

How a medical marijuana card works for three real guys.

In November 1996, California became the first state to legalize medical cannabis. (Many) politics and (some) physicians promised us breakthroughs in better health. Thirty years later, how are we really doing? Men’s Health decided to find out in this special report package. Read more here.

THREE REAL GUYS. Three dramatically different mental health conditions. Here’s how a weed card works for them.

THE GUY: TJ, a 47-year-old data insights professional

THE CONDITION: Multiple sclerosis, a chronic autoimmune disease that affects the central nervous system

THE EXPERIENCE: TJ was diagnosed with MS at the age of 25. Doctors prescribed him Rebif, a drug that helped his symptoms but made him depressed. “I was immediately suicidal on that medicine,” he says. Years before medical marijuana was legalized in his state, a friend suggested pot. TJ was desperate, tried weed, and says he felt immediate relief. In 2022, he obtained his medical card. Today, TJ struggles with nerve pain and migraines but says cannabis helps him play volleyball, travel, and stay active. “I don’t know that I would have a productive and happy life if I were not treating myself that way.”

THE GUY: CJ, a 32-year-old postdoctoral researcher of addiction neurobiology

THE CONDITION: Sarcoidosis, an inflammatory disease that causes collections of immune cells, called granulomas, to form in the body. Depending on the location, granulomas can result in fatigue, joint pain, nausea, and swelling. CJ has a rare form located in his brain stem.

THE EXPERIENCE: CJ’s symptoms began in his early 20s, starting with skin issues and leading, eventually, to debilitating nausea. After doctors diagnosed him with sarcoidosis in 2021, he went from using cannabis occasionally to using it daily for nausea control. Through self-experimentation, he learned the combination of cannabinoids that worked the best. Because the ratios were harder to find in OTC products, CJ got his medical card in 2023. “This brought down my overall symptom load a huge amount,” CJ says. Along with an off-label Crohn’s disease medication, medical marijuana has brought him back to 90 percent of his old self, he says.

THE GUY: Jonah, a 41-year-old bookstore manager

THE CONDITION: Generalized anxiety disorder (GAD), a mental health condition that causes excessive fear and worry and interferes with day-to-day functioning.

THE EXPERIENCE: Jonah says he was always labeled “the weird kid.” He ran away at 16 and experienced problems with drugs, alcohol, and homelessness in his early 20s, until his mother convinced him to talk to a therapist, who diagnosed him with anxiety and recommended medical marijuana. After using cannabis for a few weeks, Jonah says, the dark fog that had made him want to act out or isolate himself began to lift. “Conversations with people became a lot easier; decision-making, especially good decisions, became a lot easier.” Sixteen years after that therapy appointment, he’s happily married and life is good, he says.

There’s so much more going on in the world of weed and health. Read more here.

Lauren Vinopal is a writer based in Chicago whose work has been published in GQ, Slate, Mel Magazine, and more.

#4123. The secret to slower aging may be hiding in your gut

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Scientists have uncovered a surprising connection between the bacteria living in the human gut and the speed at which the body ages. Researchers at the University of Hawaiʻi at Mānoa found that certain gut microbes are associated with slower biological aging, while others are linked to faster aging.

The study, published in Scientific Reports, found that differences in gut bacterial communities accounted for approximately 15% of the variation in participants' biological aging rates. The results suggest that the gut microbiome, the vast community of microorganisms living in the digestive system, may provide valuable clues about aging at the molecular level.

"This study advances our understanding of the relationship between the gut microbiome and biological aging," said senior author Alika K. Maunakea of the UH Mānoa John A. Burns School of Medicine. "We found that microbial patterns in the gut were significantly associated with molecular measures of aging, even independent of chronological age. These findings open important new directions for understanding how modifiable biological systems influence long-term health trajectories and longevity."

Scientists Identify Gut Bacteria Linked to Faster and Slower Aging

To investigate the relationship between gut bacteria and aging, researchers examined stool and blood samples collected from 123 adults across a wide range of ages. They identified the bacteria present in each participant's gut and compared those findings with blood-based molecular markers used to estimate the body's rate of aging.

These biological measurements differ from chronological age, which simply reflects how many years someone has been alive. Instead, they are designed to assess how quickly aging-related changes are occurring within the body.

The researchers found a notable association between the composition of the gut microbiome and DunedinPACE, an advanced biomarker that measures the pace of biological aging.

Some bacterial species stood out for their particularly strong relationships with aging. Bifidobacterium adolescentis was among the microbes most strongly associated with slower biological aging. In contrast, Succinivibrio dextrinosolvens showed one of the strongest associations with faster aging.

These differences suggest that particular patterns of gut bacteria may offer important information about how rapidly the body is aging.

How Diet, Lifestyle, and Environment May Influence Gut Health

Lead author Braden P. Kunihiro said the discovery adds to growing evidence that the gut microbiome could play an important role in precision health research, which aims to better understand individual differences in health and disease.

Unlike chronological age, the composition of the gut microbiome can change in response to everyday influences, including diet, lifestyle, and environmental conditions. This makes it a particularly interesting area of investigation for scientists studying healthy aging.

"The gut microbiome is highly responsive to lifestyle, diet, environment and other everyday exposures," Kunihiro said. "Our findings suggest that the microbiome may reflect important aspects of biological resilience and aging in ways that could eventually help inform future wellness and healthy aging strategies."

The research also included Native Hawaiian and Pacific Islander participants, groups that have historically received limited representation in studies of aging and gut microorganisms.

Co-author Ruben Juarez explained that including these populations could help scientists investigate how social circumstances and environmental conditions leave lasting biological effects over the course of a person's life.

"The microbiome exists at the intersection of behavior, environment and human biology," Juarez said. "Studies like this help illuminate potential pathways through which lived experiences and social factors influence long-term health outcomes."

Could Changing Gut Bacteria Influence How We Age?

Although the findings reveal a meaningful relationship between gut bacteria and biological aging, researchers stress that the study does not establish cause and effect. In other words, finding certain microbes in people who age more slowly does not necessarily mean those bacteria are responsible for slowing the aging process.

Further studies will be needed to determine whether deliberately changing the gut microbiome can influence biological aging or improve long-term health.

Still, the findings offer a promising starting point for exploring how the microorganisms living inside us relate to the aging process and whether they could eventually help guide more personalized approaches to healthy aging.

"This is an important early step toward understanding the gut microbiome's relationship with healthy aging," Maunakea said. "Future research will help clarify the mechanisms involved and whether these microbial signatures may eventually contribute to precision approaches for promoting long-term health and wellness."

Materials provided by University of Hawaii at Manoa. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#4110. Low testosterone may make sugar far more harmful to your liver

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Low testosterone can contribute to several health problems, and an unhealthy diet may make some of those risks even worse. One major concern is metabolic dysfunction-associated steatotic liver disease (MASLD), a condition that affects approximately 40% of adult men worldwide and has become a significant global health issue.

Fatty liver, the earliest stage of MASLD, occurs when excess fat builds up in the liver. Several factors are associated with the condition, including obesity, type 2 diabetes, low testosterone levels, and excessive fructose consumption from sugary drinks and processed foods. However, scientists have not fully understood how these factors interact or whether their combined effects are more harmful than each one individually.

How Low Testosterone and Fructose Affect the Liver

To investigate this connection, a research team from the Graduate School of Agriculture, led by graduate student Hiroki Takahashi and Associate Professor Naoki Harada, examined how reduced testosterone and high fructose intake influence liver health.

The researchers used eight-week-old male mice and divided them into six experimental groups. Some underwent castration to lower testosterone levels, while others received a sham operation. The groups included Sham/Control, Sham/Fructose, Sham/Fructose + Antibiotics, Castration/Control, Castration/Fructose, and Castration/Fructose + Antibiotics.

To determine how these conditions affected the animals, the team analyzed liver cells and tissues, blood plasma, organic acids from the cecum, and samples of gut bacteria.

The results revealed that mice with reduced testosterone levels developed heavier livers when they consumed fructose. However, this increase in liver weight was smaller in mice that also received antibiotics.

When examined separately, low testosterone and fructose intake produced only modest changes in liver triglyceride levels. When combined, however, the two factors had a much stronger effect, significantly increasing fat accumulation and worsening fatty liver.

The researchers also identified changes in the composition of gut bacteria, altered liver gene activity, and elevated levels of pyruvate in the cecum among mice exposed to both reduced testosterone and fructose.

These findings suggested that the gut microbiome could play an important role in explaining why the combination was particularly harmful.

Further experiments helped the researchers identify a possible biological explanation for the increased liver fat.

"Upon examining this mechanism, we found that changes in the gut microbiota led to increased levels of pyruvate within the intestine. Furthermore, experiments using mouse-derived primary hepatocytes revealed that pyruvate acts synergistically with fructose to promote neutral lipid accumulation in hepatocytes," explained Takahashi.

In other words, changes in gut bacteria appeared to increase intestinal pyruvate, a compound involved in energy metabolism. Laboratory experiments with liver cells isolated from mice showed that pyruvate and fructose worked together to increase fat storage in those cells.

The discovery suggests that interactions among testosterone levels, dietary fructose, and gut bacteria may help explain how fatty liver develops and worsens.

Potential New Ways to Prevent Fatty Liver Disease

Understanding this relationship could eventually help researchers identify new approaches to preventing or treating fatty liver disease. In particular, targeting the processes through which pyruvate contributes to fat accumulation may offer promising possibilities.

"Going forward, we hope to clarify the mechanism by which pyruvate promotes triglyceride accumulation to pioneer the development of therapeutic drugs and establishment of preventive methods through dietary interventions," added Professor Harada.

Although the findings come from experiments in mice and isolated mouse liver cells, they provide new insight into how hormonal changes and diet may interact to influence liver health.

The study was published in American Journal of Physiology-Endocrinology and Metabolism.

Materials provided by Osaka Metropolitan University. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#3426. Microplastics Are Everywhere. Don’t Make It Worse

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Our expert, award-winning staff selects the products we cover and rigorously researches and tests our top picks. If you buy through our links, we may earn a commission.

Don't let your microwave become a hotbed for microplastics.

Standing near your microwave almost certainly won’t give you cancer, but that doesn’t mean the popular kitchen appliance isn’t linked to health concerns. While glass, ceramic and porcelain are safe to use in a microwave, certain food storage vessels present a clear and present danger when nuked at such high temperatures.

The microwave is an undoubtedly handy tool for reheating leftovers — though better options exist — softening butter, or even quickly cooking meats with this surprisingly effective microwave grill plate, but what you put in the microwave matters to avoid the risk of ingesting microplastics.

Study after study has shown that the resulting chemical exposure from microplastics could pose significant health risks, and they have been linked to increases in medical issues like infertility and even cancer. Cheap plastic containers can leach microplastics into food, and that risk increases when you heat them in the microwave.

Read more: Is Your Kitchen Full of Microplastics? Swap These 7 Tools Before Your Next Meal

Here’s everything you need to know about microwaving plastic.

Most black plastic containers are not microwave-safe.

“If you’re using takeout containers to reheat food, they need to be labeled as heat-safe,” advises Ricardo Jarquin, executive chef of Loews Coral Gables in Miami. “Most restaurants do not use them because they’re more expensive.”

Aluminum, cardboard and paper-based containers are typically plastic-free and safe for storing and reheating food. Cheap plastic takeout containers are often unsafe and should never be used to reheat food. Styrofoam is even worse because it melts in the microwave, a clear sign it’s unsafe for reheating food.

Read more: These 7 Takeout Containers Don’t Belong in the Recycling

It might sound like more work, but bringing your own food storage container for leftovers is the safest bet.

So what’s the best way to reheat that delicious meal? We chatted with Jarquin, who has overseen culinary operations and food safety protocols for major hotel chains including Four Seasons, Hilton, and Marriott, and Hope Montgomery, owner of Brick & Mortar in St. Petersburg, Florida, to get the skinny on safely using takeout containers.

Ideally, the restaurant is using compostable paper, aluminum or cardboard products, which can be found at Brick & Mortar and is Montgomery’s material of choice. A surefire way to ensure food is stored and reheated in a heat-safe container (whether by initial temperature or via microwave) is to use your own.

“The safest containers are BPA-free, or even better, glass ones like Pyrex,” recommends Jarquin. “Stainless steel and aluminum are great, too, just not for the microwave.”

One of our favorite options is this colorful 10-piece set that offers the convenience of multiple sizes. This means you can choose the right shape for what you plan to reheat or take home, from an entire entree to a few bites of dessert.

Many plastic containers are marketed as “freezer- and microwave-safe,” but that’s not the case.

Many plastic containers are marketed as “freezer- and microwave-safe,” but that’s mostly marketing jargon. Plastic containers should really never be frozen or microwaved. Ziploc and Rubbermaid both faced a 2025 class-action lawsuit for advertising their plastic containers as “microwave-safe” and “freezer-safe.” When heated, plastic containers can leach chemicals into food. When frozen, the plastic often breaks off, which can contaminate food.

Deli containers may be considered food-safe, but glass and ceramic are safer options for reheating food in the microwave.

More important than reheating food so it tastes good is making sure it’s safe to eat. Never reheat your leftovers in a non-heat-safe vessel. That includes most cheap plastic containers restaurants use, such as plastic clamshells or black plastic containers. Deli containers like the ones you get your grocery store chicken salad in are considered “food safe” for storage, but it’s still not recommended to use them to reheat food at high temperatures.

When reheating food in the microwave, always use glass or ceramic. Keep any containers, lids or utensils that contain plastic or rubber out and use for serving only.

An air fryer is one of our favorite ways to reheat leftovers.

Beyond the risks of plastic exposure when hot food is stored in those containers, you’ll want to ensure reheating leftovers maintains overall quality.

“Though microwaving is quick and easy, better results can often be achieved by reheating on the stovetop,” says Montgomery. “A little extra fat like olive oil and butter, as well as seasoning, can also be helpful,” she adds, noting that this doesn’t pertain to some dishes like her restaurant’s famed beef tenderloin carpaccio, which should be consumed in its entirety on location.

Jarquin echoes these sentiments. “You get a much better result when reheating leftovers in a frying pan or in the oven,” he stresses, adding that some dishes may even taste better the next day. “At Americana Kitchen, the rigatoni with vodka sauce keeps getting tastier the next day. Also, our pizzas and mushroom pasta definitely get better the next day. The flavors soak in even more and the texture stays great when reheated slowly,” he says.

Jarquin also recommends enjoying at-home foods like “stews, curries, chili, and lasagna,” where flavors can keep developing overnight.

Food, including cooked rice, can develop bacteria quickly if not stored properly.

Another risk with leftovers is the rapid growth of bacteria, which can cause brief but sometimes serious illnesses. In short, the longer food sits out, the quicker these bacteria grow.

“The general rule is that it takes about two hours at room temperature and one hour if it’s really hot out”  (usually above 90 degrees Fahrenheit), said Jarquin. “After that, bacteria can grow quickly, and you don’t always see or smell it. That’s why it’s important to either serve food hot, chill it fast or keep it properly stored.”

Read more: I’m Not Messing Around With Leftover Rice — You Shouldn’t Either

At the end of the day, if you’re even slightly fearful of microplastics and/or foodborne illnesses, no leftover is worth the temporary satisfaction. Accept your losses and whip up your own homemade delicacy to ensure safe, plastic-free bites in the comfort of your own kitchen.

For a comprehensive guide to food safety protocols, check out the US Centers for Disease Control’s Four Steps to Food Safety.

The information contained in this article is for educational and informational purposes only and is not intended as health or medical advice. Always consult a physician or other qualified health provider regarding any questions you may have about a medical condition or health objectives.

#4516. Healthgrades Announces Top Hospitals for Specialty Care, Outpatient Procedures

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

Hospital ratings have become an important resource for choosing where to receive care. They influence which doctors patients choose, how far they are willing to travel, and whether they seek care at a different facility.

On October 27, Healthgrades announced the recipients of the 2027 Specialty Excellence Outpatient Awards and Specialty State Rankings.

The annual awards recognize hospitals delivering outstanding patient outcomes in specialty care across the United States. Healthgrades is the leading platform used by Americans for finding a doctor or hospital.

New research from Healthgrades highlights that hospital quality is increasingly a factor in healthcare decisions.

Around 80% of patients report that when looking for a doctor, the quality of the hospital where they practice matters. Additionally, 7 in 10 patients (68%) say they’re more likely to seek care for a procedure or health condition from a doctor who works at a hospital with high quality ratings.

What’s more, around 71% of patients say they would switch doctors to gain access to a higher-rated hospital.

“Our specialty awards identify the nation’s top-performing hospitals across numerous medical specialties, helping consumers find high quality care tailored to their specific needs,” said Alana Biggers, MD, MPH, medical advisor at Healthgrades.

Healthgrades is owned by RVO Health. By clicking on this link, we may receive a commission. Learn more.

“Navigating the complex healthcare system can feel daunting, but taking a proactive stance to understand where to access superior care can enhance your healthcare journey and overall experience. The ability to make informed decisions about your health — and where to get care — is a cornerstone of self-advocacy, and we are proud to help make that information accessible to all patients.”

Healthgrades identifies hospitals that deliver exceptional outcomes across common outpatient procedures and specialty conditions.

Finding high quality care is important, particularly amid a growing gap between top-performing and one-star hospitals.

According to Healthgrades, from 2023 to 2025, if all hospitals had performed at the five-star level from 2023 to 2025:

“One of the biggest challenges is balancing increasing patient needs with the resources available to healthcare professionals,” Biggers said.

“Hospitals face workforce shortages, growing administrative responsibilities, financial pressures, and increasing complexity in patient care. Physicians and other healthcare professionals need adequate time and support not only to evaluate and treat patients, but also to communicate results, coordinate follow-up care, and address concerns that arise between visits,” she continued.

“When healthcare teams are adequately supported, they are better positioned to communicate effectively with patients, coordinate care, and address patients’ needs.”

Biggers said that ensuring equal access to quality healthcare is challenging because of factors such as:

Hospital closures and reduced services make access even more difficult. Biggers identified a few possible solutions, such as expanding outpatient care, building community partnerships, using telehealth when appropriate, and improving coordination among healthcare providers.

“Ultimately, providing high quality care requires more than clinical expertise. It requires healthcare systems to invest in their workforce, strengthen care coordination, and create environments where healthcare professionals have the resources they need to provide safe, effective, and patient-centered care,” Biggers said.

“Equally important, healthcare systems must ensure that patients have meaningful access to these services, regardless of where they live or their socioeconomic circumstances.”

Biggers said that outstanding hospitals recognize that high quality care extends beyond just treating an illness.

“It involves listening to patients, understanding their individual circumstances, and ensuring they feel respected, informed, and involved in their healthcare decisions,” she said.

Patients want to find a hospital that excels in the procedures or health conditions relevant to them.

The recipients of the Specialty Excellence Awards are among the top 15% of hospitals specializing in these areas.

Healthgrades evaluated patient outcomes at roughly 4,500 hospitals across 31 common procedures and conditions to reach their decisions.

Among the list of Specialty Excellence Award recipients, Healthgrades also recognizes America’s 100 Best Hospitals for Specialty Care for 11 specialty areas andAmerica’s 50 Best Hospitals for Specialty Care for four specialty areas.

“An outstanding hospital prioritizes both patient care and the people delivering that care,” Biggers told Healthline.

“While clinical outcomes, patient safety, and access to evidence-based treatments are essential measures of quality, I believe hospitals must also invest in workforce development and support. This means ensuring physicians, nurses, and other healthcare professionals have appropriate staffing levels, adequate clinical and administrative support, and opportunities for continued professional development,” she continued.

Healthgrades awards U.S. hospitals that excel at Outpatient Orthopedic Surgery, Outpatient Joint Replacement, andOutpatient Prostate Care.

Outpatient care is one of the fastest-growing areas of healthcare. The number of orthopedic procedures performed in an outpatient environment has grown exponentially in recent years.

From 2020 to 2025, outpatient hip replacement procedures grew by 81%, while inpatient procedures dropped by 75%. Outpatient knee replacements increased by 71% while inpatient volumes decreased by 70%.

“I believe several factors are contributing to the growing demand for outpatient care, including advances in medical technology, a greater emphasis on preventive care, and the increasing need to manage chronic medical conditions,” Biggers said.

“Many services and procedures that previously required hospitalization can now be safely performed in outpatient settings. Patients also appreciate the convenience of receiving care closer to home and avoiding unnecessary hospital stays,” she added.

To meet the increasing demand for outpatient procedures, Biggers cautioned that healthcare systems must ensure that these services are adequately staffed and supported.

“Effective communication and coordination between outpatient providers, specialists, and hospitals are essential to maintaining continuity and quality of care,” she said.

State Rankings identify the top three or five hospitals in eligible states across 19 key specialty areas.

Healthgrades is owned by RVO Health. By clicking on this link, we may receive a commission. Learn more.

State-level specialty care rankings help consumers find outstanding local hospitals for their condition or procedure.

The analysis used Medicare inpatient data from the Centers for Medicare & Medicaid Services (CMS) from 2023 to 2025, and assessed patient outcomes across 31 conditions and procedures.

These outcomes served as the foundation for determining Specialty Excellence Awards and state hospital rankings.

With consumer-facing awards and ratings influencing healthcare decisions, transparency about their methods and limitations remains essential.

Ratings can help patients ask better questions and compare options, but they should complement — not replace — clinical guidance and informed decision making.

#5312. <a href="https://www.fiercehealthcare.com/providers/hhs-calls-hospitals-sign-make-hospital-food-healthier-pledge" hreflang="en">Over 250 hospitals sign HHS' voluntary 'Make Hospital Food Healthier Pledge'</a>

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →
Over 250 hospitals sign HHS' voluntary 'Make Hospital Food Healthier Pledge'" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

More than 250 hospitals across the country have signed onto a voluntary pledge circulated by the Department of Health and Human Services to serve healthier food to their patients.

The tally, shared late Monday, includes early big-name adopter systems like Sanford Health, Corewell Health and Houston Methodist as well as more recent signatories like Cleveland Clinic, SSM Health, HonorHealth, Parkview Health, WellSpan Health and Nemours Children's, among others.

“Hospitals have a responsibility to promote health in every part of patient care, including the food they serve,” HHS Secretary Robert F. Kennedy Jr. said in a release. “More than 250 hospitals are taking action to improve nutrition for their patients, staff and communities. I am calling on every hospital in America to join them, sign the pledge, and help make healthy food a standard part of healthcare.”

HHS paired its announcement with the unveiling of a new badge hospitals that have signed the pledge may display to patients on their websites. Participating organizations commit to work toward eliminating deep-fried foods, limiting sugar-sweetened beverages and winding down foods high in added sugars, sodium or other artificial additives.

The department and its secretary, Robert F. Kennedy Jr., launched the pledge in early July, and a month later earned the endorsement of the American Hospital Association (see those stories below). It lands alongside other healthcare nutrition-focused initiatives like revised guidance from the Centers for Medicare and Medicaid Services and the integration of nutrition content in medical education, efforts that have been taken up by others in the industry.

The Department of Health and Human Services' push to bring healthier food to hospitals got an endorsement Friday from the country's top hospital lobby and some of its most prominent members.

American Hospital Association President and CEO Rick Pollack said in a statement that his group supports the voluntary pledge launched by HHS in early July (see that story below). It includes commitments to eliminate deep-fried foods, “limit” sugar-sweetened beverages and broadly wind down foods high in added sugars, sodium or other artificial additives—which officials including HHS Secretary Robert F. Kennedy, Jr., have said will aid recovery and more broadly help address chronic disease burden.

AHA, Pollack said, is "committed to helping hospitals—as clinically appropriate and feasible—ensure that patients have access to nourishing meals, including by sharing this voluntary pledge with the hospital field and encouraging them to review it and give it serious consideration.”

Additionally, HHS announced that its has received signatures from three major health systems whose leaders are the past, present and upcoming chairs of the AHA: Corewell Health, Houston Methodist and Sanford Health.

“Hospitals cannot promote healing while serving food that contributes to chronic disease,” Kennedy said in the Friday announcement. "By joining this pledge, these health systems are putting nutrition where it belongs—at the center of patient care. We are working with hospitals across America to serve healthier food, improve patient health, and Make America Healthy Again.”

Nutrition advocacy has been a spot of agreement between Kennedy and a broader healthcare provider community that has vehemently criticized his other priorities, such as changes to childhood vaccine schedules.

The Department of Health and Human Services and the Centers for Medicare and Medicaid Services want hospitals to take a voluntary step toward serving healthier food to their patients.

The “Make Hospital Food Healthier Pledge” unveiled this week is the latest in Secretary Robert F. Kennedy, Jr.’s and other health officials’ emphasis on nutrition as a factor in chronic disease prevention and general long-term wellness.

Though voluntary, it follows concrete commitments the administration has scored from dozens of educational institutions to bring nutrition into the medical curriculum.

“Patients recovering from serious medical conditions deserve better than ultra-processed and deep-fried junk foods,” Kennedy said in HHS’ announcement of the pledge. “President Trump has directed HHS to put real food at the center of American health. Today, we’re challenging hospitals across the country to lead by example by serving nutritious, minimally processed meals that help patients heal, reduce chronic disease, and help Make America Healthy Again.”

HHS began pointing hospitals in this direction back in March, when CMS issued a Quality and Safety Special Alert Memo underscoring Medicare Conditions of Participation (CoP) that require in-house or contracted food delivery “in accordance with recognized dietary practices.” Recommendations included in the memo were in line with HHS and the Department of Agriculture’s overhauled dietary guidelines, which discourage “ultraprocessed” foods and prioritize foods with proteins, healthy fats and whole grains.

The Make Hospital Food Healthier Pledge hits each of those beats with additional commitments to eliminate deep-fried foods, “limit” sugar-sweetened beverages and broadly wind down foods high in added sugars, sodium or other artificial additives.

Doing so will help hospitals “improve patient outcomes, reduce complications, and promote healthier communities long after patients leave their care,” according to the pledge’s website.

“Hospitals should nourish patients with the same commitment they bring to every other aspect of care,” CMS Administrator Mehmet Oz, M.D., said in the pledge announcement. “That’s why we’re challenging hospitals to limit ultra-processed foods, feature nutritious meals that promote healing and lead the way in delivering prevention-first, whole-person care.”

Outside of a “farm-to-hospital” food sourcing initiative at Nicklaus Children’s Hospital that officials highlighted back in March, HHS has not named any other hospitals that have made similar voluntary healthy food commitments. The webpage for HHS’ new hospital pledge does not display the names of any organizations that have signed on.

Still, nutrition advocacy has been a spot of agreement between Kennedy and a broader healthcare provider community that has vehemently criticized his other priorities, such as changes to childhood vaccine schedules. Oz has also been upfront about his agency directing “significantly more” of the Rural Health Transformation Fund’s $50 billion to states that heavily featured nutrition education criteria as part of their applications.

#4544. In 1828, serial killers sold their victims’ bodies to science

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Published Oct 27, 2026 8:53 AM EDT

For as long as surgeons cut open living people to keep them alive, they have needed dead people to practice on. Today, donation programs keep medical schools supplied with the bodies they need to teach their students. But in 1820s Scotland, donating your body to science wasn’t legal yet. Nonetheless, one doctor found a way to get the bodies he needed. But it came at a terrible cost. This is the grisly story of the Anatomy Murders.

In the early 1800s, surgery was booming. A few decades earlier, Scottish surgeon John Hunter had helped bring a scientific view to Western medicine, transforming surgery with experimentation and careful observation. The Napoleonic Wars had boosted the demand for trained surgeons, Alun Evans, a retired epidemiologist at Queen’s University in Belfast, tells Popular Science.

One of the leading lights in this burgeoning field was Hunter’s compatriot Robert Knox, who ran an anatomy school in Edinburgh. But by 1827, Knox had hit a roadblock to his teaching ambitions. A change to regulations meant that dissection had become an important part of the medical curriculum. Knox’s school would require more than 400 bodies the following year. What made this such an overwhelming ask was that citizens had no legal way to donate their bodies to medical science. But where there’s a demand, supply will follow.

Edinburgh Medical School first opened in 1728, and for much of the next century, allegations of grave robbing linked to the school had been rife. Newly buried corpses would be exhumed and then presented at medical school doors by so-called “resurrectionists”.

The provenance of these bodies was unimportant to the corpse-strapped medical profession. But families, desperate to protect their loved ones, had taken extreme steps to stamp out the practice. Relatives would stay at their loved ones’ graveside for weeks, waiting for their bodies to lose their medical value. Factories produced devices like mort safes, heavy iron cages placed over newly dug graves. Knox had been forced to source his bodies from further away, even bringing some from across the Irish Sea.

Across the city, two other men also had a body problem. Irishman William Hare owned a lodging house with his wife near Edinburgh’s Grassmarket. One of his tenants was a fellow Irishman, William Burke. Burke and Hare became friends.

One day in early 1828, Hare discovered one of his tenants had died overnight. Even worse, the dead man still owed him rent. The pair came up with a plan to recoup the money, and they carried the corpse to Knox’s door. There, he paid them £7 and 10 shillings; more than a month’s wages.

Their eyes opened by greed, Burke and Hare hatched a plan to get Knox the bodies he desperately needed. Over the next 12 months, they brought him 16 fresh corpses. They had acquired each one by murdering their way through the poor of Edinburgh. They had lured the victims to Hare’s guesthouse, and then suffocated them in the night, lying on their chests to minimize any damage to their valuable bodies. The majority of their victims were women.

Burke and Hare’s rampage was only stopped in November 1828. A tenant at Hare’s guesthouse reported to the police that they had found a corpse under their bed. When the police raided the premises, they only found a single spot of blood where the body had been.

But under interrogation, Burke and his wife gave inconsistent accounts. Eventually, Burke, Hare, and their wives were all arrested.

Despite their equal guilt in their crimes, the two men had very different fates. Hare offered to confess everything to the authorities in exchange for his freedom. His evidence condemned Burke.

Two months later, Burke was hanged in front of a baying crowd. Ironically, his crimes meant that, under the Murder Act of 1752, he was not allowed a burial. His body was instead donated to the Edinburgh Medical School, where his skeleton is still on show today.

Hare disappeared after being freed. Evans says that research suggests he returned to Ireland, where some think he ended his days in Kilkeel Workhouse in County Down.

Knox faced graver consequences than Hare. He was never tried for his link to the murders, but his reputation was buried. He was forced out of a curatorial role at the Royal College of Surgeons and eventually moved to London. He was unable to secure another university teaching post, but continued to work as an anatomist at a hospital in the city.

Evans says the taint of the scandal went beyond Knox. “The Burke and Hare scandal really stymied medicine in Edinburgh considerably, and it explained the rising popularity of the Dublin schools and the Paris schools of medicine,” he says.

One fact that may have haunted Knox is that if he had avoided the lure of Burke and Hare’s corpses for just a few more years, he would have benefited from the Anatomy Act, passed in 1832, which gave surgeons the green light to procure any bodies from hospitals, prisons, or workhouses that had gone unclaimed for 48 hours. Voluntary donation was also legalized at the same time.

Instead, Knox became part of a black legend in Scotland’s history. Burke and Hare’s crimes have become an indelible part of Edinburgh’s grim past.

#5284. STAT+: Pennsylvania Dutch country, tired of waiting for biopharma, builds drug company of its own

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Test your knowledge with our new weekday mini crossword

Jason is a general assignment reporter, with particular focus on genetic medicine and rare disease. Confidential tips can be sent on Signal at JasonMast.77.

LANCASTER, Pa. — They saved Petunia first. She was the second cow. Her brother Louie had died within hours of his birth. But over two harrowing, sleepless nights, the three researchers kept Petunia alive to wheel her from the barn to the veterinary hospital and inject her with a virus carrying new genes.

Petunia had a bovine form of maple syrup urine disease (MSUD), a life-threatening condition that is also unusually prevalent within the Mennonite community here. The goal wasn’t to save the 79-pound calf. That would be a bonus. The goal, for Kevin Strauss, was to prove that an MSUD gene therapy could work — that after two decades he could finally offer the desperate families who entered his medical clinic here a simple, one-time fix.

It worked. Today, Petunia is a feisty, 5-year-old, hay-munching, 1,500 pound heifer. Yet not a single one of Strauss’s patients has received the treatment that saved her life, not even in a clinical trial.
