#297. Myanmar’s Aung San Suu Kyi ‘sounds strong’ but is isolated, son says

📅 Oct 25, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Kim Aris tells the Guardian chance to speak with detained Nobel laureate was ‘public relations’ move from military leaders

Myanmar’s former democratic leader Aung San Suu Kyi appears to be in good physical and mental health but is living in near-complete isolation, her son has said after the first call with his 81-year-old mother in more than five years.

“She still sounds certainly all there mentally, and physically she sounds strong,” Kim Aris said. “I don’t think she has anybody outside of the military or the police around her, so essentially she’s been held in solitary confinement from what I gather.”

After being deposed in a 2021 military coup, the Nobel Peace prize laureate has been moved to an unspecified location in the capital. “I’m still not entirely sure where she is,” said Aris. “I think it’s a residence in Naypyidaw, which is fenced off and it has a scrubby bit of land around it.”

Aris, who has lived in the UK most of his life and whose late father was a British historian, was allowed to speak to his mother on Thursday at the Myanmar embassy in London. While he welcomed the chance to talk, he said the invitation was part of a “public relations” campaign by the country’s military leaders.

The former junta chief Min Aung Hlaing has sought to rebuild international diplomatic ties, and in April switched his military fatigues for civilian clothes after elections that were widely condemned as a sham.

Activists have long demanded “proof of life” for Aung San Suu Kyi, as well as scores of other political prisoners. In August, she was finally photographed during a Red Cross visit. Last week, a government spokesperson published images of her smiling in a wood-panelled room as officials delivered two gardening books given to her as gifts by Britain’s King Charles. A paper calendar on the wall behind her showed the date.

Aris said these photo-ops were well-known stunts that the regime had used before to pretend his mother, a national icon, was being treated fairly. The daughter of the assassinated national independence hero Gen Aung San has spent much of her life since the late 1980s in and out of house arrest for leading pro-democracy movements.

“She remains completely shut off from the rest of the world. I think she’s unaware of what’s happening outside of where she is because she’s not allowed any newspapers or radio or television,” Aris said. “She’s been locked up for five years on trumped-up, politically motivated charges and she should be free.”

Ruled for decades by generals and suffering from western sanctions, Myanmar experienced a brief democratic moment after Aung San Suu Kyi was allowed to run in a 2015 election that her party overwhelmingly won. She took up the role of “state counsellor” under a civilian-military power-sharing agreement.

Her international reputation was soon tarnished after the 2016-2017 mass killings and expulsions of Rohingya Muslims. Aung San Suu Kyi flew to the international court of justice in The Hague, where she said she acknowledged military units might have used “disproportionate force”, but defended the government against accusations of genocide.

Aris said that his mother was never “silent” on the Rohingya crisis and what she said on the issue “could fill volumes”. Under her leadership, Myanmar launched a domestic inquiry and appointed the former UN chief Kofi Annan to head a commission.

He added that as state counsellor, Aung San Suu Kyi was “always walking on a tightrope”, knowing the military could strip her of her fragile power at any moment.

“The very hard-won nascent democracy that she was trying to lead was holding on to things by a thread,” he said. “The military could have taken over at any point, and they were doing their best to make my mother’s situation a lot more difficult and to undermine her position. And I think in that respect they did a pretty good job.”

The 2021 power grab by the military triggered a civil war that has killed more than 100,000 people, according to conflict monitors.

Aris said the level of public global support for his mother had “dwindled incredibly” since the Rohingya crisis and that the whole country was now suffering from a lack of international attention. While many governments have called for Aung San Suu Kyi’s release, she is not treated as the beloved international figure that she once was.

“Never mind my mother, the fact that people ignore what’s happening in Burma shouldn’t be down to what was being done to the Rohingya,” he said, using the country’s former name. “And that seems to be the case sometimes, that people say: ‘Oh, well, look how the Rohingya were treated. We can ignore what’s happening in Burma.’”

#4236. Mediterranean diet may boost tiny proteins that protect the heart and brain

📅 Oct 25, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

A new study suggests that the Mediterranean diet may protect the heart, brain, and aging cells through a surprising biological mechanism involving tiny proteins produced by mitochondria, the structures responsible for generating most of the energy our cells need.

Researchers at the USC Leonard Davis School of Gerontology discovered that people who more closely followed the Mediterranean diet had higher levels of two mitochondrial microproteins called humanin and SHMOOSE. Both have previously been linked to protection against cardiovascular disease and neurodegeneration, the gradual deterioration of nerve cells that occurs in conditions such as Alzheimer's disease.

The study, led by USC Leonard Davis Instructional Associate Professor of Gerontology Roberto Vicinanza, offers a possible explanation for how everyday food choices influence cellular health and the aging process.

"These microproteins may act as molecular messengers that translate what we eat into how our cells function and age," Vicinanza said. "It's a new biological pathway that helps explain why the Mediterranean diet is so powerful."

How the Mediterranean Diet May Protect Aging Cells

The Mediterranean diet emphasizes foods such as olive oil, fish, beans, lentils, fruits, and vegetables. It has consistently been associated with a lower risk of heart disease, type 2 diabetes, and cognitive decline. However, scientists are still working to understand exactly how this eating pattern produces its wide-ranging health benefits.

To investigate, the researchers examined blood samples from older adults who followed the Mediterranean diet to different degrees. Those whose eating habits most closely matched the diet had significantly higher concentrations of humanin and SHMOOSE.

They also showed lower indicators of oxidative stress, a process in which harmful molecules called reactive oxygen species overwhelm the body's protective defenses. Over time, oxidative stress can damage proteins, DNA, and other cellular components, contributing to aging and chronic disease.

Interestingly, the researchers found that certain foods were associated with different microproteins. Higher consumption of olive oil, fish, and legumes was linked to increased humanin levels. Meanwhile, olive oil consumption and eating fewer refined carbohydrates were associated with higher levels of SHMOOSE.

Refined carbohydrates include foods made with heavily processed grains and sugars, such as white bread, pastries, and many sugary snacks. These foods generally contain less fiber and fewer naturally occurring nutrients than their minimally processed counterparts.

"These findings suggest that specific components of the Mediterranean diet may directly influence mitochondrial biology," said USC Leonard Davis School Dean and USC Distinguished Professor Pinchas Cohen, the study's senior author. "Humanin and SHMOOSE could serve as biomarkers for adherence to the Mediterranean diet and have clinical significance."

Biomarkers are measurable biological indicators that can provide information about a person's health or physiological state. In this case, the researchers suggest that humanin and SHMOOSE could eventually help scientists understand how closely someone's diet aligns with healthy eating patterns and how their cells respond.

Tiny Mitochondrial Proteins With Powerful Effects

The findings extend a scientific effort spanning more than 20 years to investigate small proteins produced by mitochondria. Cohen has played a leading role in discovering and studying these molecules, which are increasingly recognized as important participants in cellular health.

Mitochondria are often described as the powerhouses of cells because they produce much of the energy required for essential biological activities. But their role extends beyond energy production. They also help regulate metabolism, cellular stress responses, and processes associated with aging.

Unlike most proteins, which are produced using instructions stored in the DNA inside the cell's nucleus, mitochondrial microproteins originate from genetic instructions within mitochondria themselves.

These instructions are found in small segments of mitochondrial DNA known as open reading frames. Some of these regions were once considered nonfunctional, but researchers have discovered that they can encode biologically active molecules.

One of the best understood is humanin, first identified by Cohen and fellow researchers in 2003. Subsequent studies have associated humanin with improved insulin sensitivity, better cardiovascular health, increased longevity, and protection of cognitive abilities.

Insulin sensitivity refers to how effectively the body's cells respond to insulin, the hormone that helps regulate blood sugar. Maintaining healthy insulin sensitivity is important for metabolic health and reducing the risk of type 2 diabetes.

Another intriguing microprotein is SHMOOSE (Small Human Mitochondrial ORF Over SErine tRNA), which was identified more recently by Cohen's research team.

SHMOOSE has attracted particular interest because of its possible role in protecting the brain. Scientists have found that a genetic variant of SHMOOSE is associated with an increased risk of Alzheimer's disease. The normal version, meanwhile, appears to help protect neurons from damage involving amyloid, a protein that can accumulate abnormally in the brains of people with Alzheimer's.

"These peptides are emerging as key regulators of aging biology," Cohen said. "They connect mitochondrial function to diseases like Alzheimer's and heart disease and now, potentially, to nutrition."

A Hidden Mechanism for Protecting the Heart

The scientists also identified a potentially important connection between humanin and an enzyme called Nox2, which helps generate reactive oxygen species.

Although these molecules have useful roles in normal cell signaling and immune defense, excessive production can contribute to oxidative stress and damage blood vessels and other tissues.

The researchers observed that people with higher humanin levels tended to have lower Nox2 activity. This relationship raises the possibility that humanin helps limit damaging oxidative processes, potentially offering an additional layer of cardiovascular protection.

Based on these findings, the researchers propose that the Mediterranean diet could support heart health in two complementary ways. The diet may help reduce oxidative stress directly while also supporting the production of mitochondrial microproteins that influence pathways involved in cellular damage.

However, the observed associations do not establish that the diet directly increases these proteins or that the proteins themselves prevent cardiovascular disease.

"This could represent a new cardioprotective mechanism of the Mediterranean diet," Vicinanza said.

Bringing the Mediterranean Diet to a Global Audience

Vicinanza's interest in the Mediterranean diet extends beyond investigating its effects inside the body. He has also worked to promote this traditional eating pattern internationally as a way to support human health, cultural heritage, and environmental sustainability.

As part of those efforts, he collaborated with the Municipality of Pollica in Italy, which is recognized as a UNESCO Mediterranean Diet emblematic community.

That collaboration helped support the establishment of the International Day of the Mediterranean Diet through the United Nations.

Observed every year on November 16, the international event is intended to draw attention to the Mediterranean diet's health benefits as well as its cultural traditions and environmental significance.

For Vicinanza, the new findings reinforce the relationship between traditional food practices and modern discoveries about how the body functions.

"We're connecting centuries-old dietary traditions with cutting-edge molecular biology," Vicinanza said. "It supports the idea that healthy eating patterns with little to no ultra-processed foods reflect how humans have eaten over long periods and may create conditions to which mitochondria -- ancient cellular organelles -- are likely adapted."

Could Tiny Proteins Help Unlock Healthier Aging?

Although the findings are promising, the researchers emphasize that the study was relatively small and observational. That means it identified relationships between dietary habits and biological measurements but could not determine whether eating a Mediterranean diet directly caused the differences in microprotein levels.

Other factors, including participants' overall health and lifestyle, could also influence the results.

Nevertheless, the discovery opens an intriguing possibility for the emerging field of precision nutrition, which seeks to develop dietary recommendations based on an individual's biological characteristics and needs.

If future research confirms that dietary changes can reliably influence humanin, SHMOOSE, and related microproteins, these molecules could potentially become useful tools for evaluating nutritional interventions and developing more personalized strategies for healthy aging.

The next step is to determine whether adopting a Mediterranean-style diet actually increases these proteins and whether those changes lead to measurable improvements in cardiovascular health, brain function, or disease risk.

"Our goal is to move from observing associations to understanding causality," Vicinanza said. "If we can harness these pathways, we may be able to design nutritional strategies that promote healthy aging at the molecular level."

The work was funded by the USC Daryl and Irwin Simon Nutrition for Alzheimer's Disease Prevention Research Fund (to Vicinanza), the Hanson-Thorell Family Research Award (to Vicinanza), National Institutes of Health grant P30AG094848 (to Cohen) and PRIN 2022 grant 000031_23_PP_PIGNATELLI_PRIN_2022-B53D23021240006 (to Pignatelli).

#559. Could lowering high blood pressure be as simple as stimulating your ear?

📅 Oct 24, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

A small device that delivers gentle electrical pulses through the ear could offer a new approach to treating stubborn high blood pressure. In a clinical trial led by researchers at Queen Mary University of London, the technology proved safe, well tolerated, and practical for patients to use at home.

Although the treatment did not significantly lower blood pressure compared with a placebo device across all participants, researchers observed encouraging results in patients with more severe hypertension. The findings suggest that stimulating nerves in the ear could eventually help certain people whose blood pressure remains elevated despite taking medication.

The results come from the SCRATCH-HTN trial, published in Hypertension, a journal of the American Heart Association. Researchers emphasize that larger studies are needed to determine whether the approach can provide meaningful benefits and which patients might respond best.

How Ear Nerve Stimulation Could Lower Blood Pressure

The experimental treatment uses a technique called transcutaneous autonomic neuromodulation (tAN), which aims to influence the autonomic nervous system. This network of nerves automatically controls essential bodily functions, including heart rate, digestion, and blood pressure.

By delivering mild electrical signals to nerves in the ear, researchers hope to influence the body's natural mechanisms for regulating blood pressure without requiring an invasive procedure.

The handheld device, developed by Afferent Medical Solutions, uses two small clips positioned on the tragus, the small flap of cartilage located just in front of the ear canal. These clips deliver gentle electrical stimulation, allowing patients to administer the treatment themselves without visiting a clinic.

Clinical Trial Tests the Device in 63 Patients

The clinical trial, funded by the National Institute for Health and Care Research (NIHR), enrolled 63 people with uncontrolled hypertension. All participants were already taking at least one medication to lower their blood pressure, but their readings remained above recommended levels.

Researchers randomly divided the participants into two groups. One received active nerve stimulation, while the other used a sham device designed to serve as a comparison. This approach helped investigators assess whether the electrical stimulation produced benefits beyond those associated with using the device itself.

Participants used their assigned devices for 30 minutes daily during the first two weeks. For the following 10 weeks, they reduced their sessions to once per week.

The results showed that the treatment was safe and generally comfortable for participants. Most also followed the prescribed schedule, suggesting that the technology could be practical for regular use at home.

However, when researchers compared blood pressure changes across the entire study population, they found no statistically significant difference between patients receiving active stimulation and those using the sham device.

Promising Results in Patients With Severe Hypertension

Despite the lack of a significant overall benefit, the researchers identified a potentially important finding among participants who entered the trial with more severe hypertension.

A prespecified subgroup analysis, meaning the researchers had planned this comparison before examining the results, revealed a greater reduction in blood pressure among these patients when they received active nerve stimulation rather than the sham treatment.

This finding raises the possibility that the technique may work better for people with particularly difficult-to-control blood pressure. However, the trial was relatively small, and the subgroup results require confirmation in larger studies before any conclusions can be drawn about its effectiveness.

Dr. Ajay Gupta, Chief Investigator, Clinical Reader at Queen Mary University of London and Consultant Physician at Barts Health NHS Trust, said:

"This is an exciting, highly patient-acceptable technology that could ultimately provide a cheap, scalable solution for millions. Although the trial did not achieve significance across the broad unselected group, those with more severe hypertension exhibited a clear blood pressure reduction in the active group compared to sham.

"As the first and most thorough study of its kind, this points towards important new research directions that could help us understand whether non-invasive neuromodulation can benefit particular groups of patients with uncontrolled hypertension."

A Potential New Option for Hard-to-Control Blood Pressure

High blood pressure, also known as hypertension, is a major contributor to cardiovascular disease and increases the risk of heart attacks and strokes. It places additional strain on blood vessels and the heart, potentially causing serious damage over time.

Although medications help many patients manage their condition, a substantial number continue to have blood pressure readings above recommended targets even while receiving treatment.

This ongoing challenge has encouraged scientists to explore additional approaches that could complement existing therapies.

Ear nerve stimulation is one such possibility. Because the device is small, does not require surgery, and can be used at home, it could offer a convenient option if future research establishes that it reliably lowers blood pressure in certain patients.

For now, the findings represent an early step rather than proof of an effective new treatment. The researchers say larger clinical trials will be essential to confirm the encouraging results in patients with severe hypertension and identify which groups, if any, could benefit most from this approach.

#496. New mRNA Vaccine Reduces Risk of Melanoma Recurrence by Over 40 Percent

📅 Oct 24, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

An experimental vaccine offers new hope to patients with melanoma, one of the most aggressive forms of skin cancer. The pharmaceutical companies Merck and Moderna released the results of a Phase 3 clinical trial that evaluated the efficacy of a personalized mRNA-based vaccine that, when combined with immunotherapy, reduced the risk of disease recurrence or death by 42 percent compared to treatment with the immunotherapy drug alone.

Georgina Long, medical director of the Australian Melanoma Institute and the study’s principal investigator, said in a statement that these results are “transformative” and “have the potential to make a foundational shift in how we treat patients with earlier-stage melanoma.”

The trial, known as INTerpath-001, included 1,137 people with high-risk melanoma who had undergone surgery to completely remove their tumors. The participants were divided into two groups. Those in the first group received only Pembrolizumab, a drug that helps the immune system detect and fight cancer cells, while those in the second group were treated with the same drug in combination with the mRNA vaccine, called Intismeran Autogene.

After monitoring the participants for an average of 24.6 months, the researchers determined that addition of the vaccine reduced the risk of cancer recurrence or death by 42 percent compared to Pembrolizumab alone.

The results also showed that the experimental treatment reduced the risk of the disease spreading to other parts of the body by 38 percent. At the end of the follow-up period, 82.2 percent of patients who received both treatments remained recurrence-free, compared with 70 percent of those who received Pembrolizumab alone.

“The results of the INTerpath-001 study represent a major breakthrough in the treatment of melanoma and, in particular, in the development of personalized medicine,” Eva Muñoz Couselo—who leads a melanoma research group at the Vall d’Hebron Institute of Oncology, in Barcelona and who was not involved in the study—told SMC Spain. “For the first time, a Phase III trial has demonstrated that an individualized therapeutic vaccine, designed on the basis of the molecular characteristics of each patient’s tumor, can significantly improve the outcomes of conventional immunotherapy.”

The laboratories explained that the vaccine was developed from a tumor sample taken from each patient, which was used to identify specific mutations that would create distinct proteins the immune system could learn to recognize in order to attack cancer cells. The scientists selected up to three of these molecular signatures per patient.

During the trial, some participants experienced mild side effects such as fever, fatigue, and pain at the injection site. These side effects were seen in earlier trials as well, and the Phase 3 findings demonstrate similar safety when the vaccine is used in the combination with Pembrolizumab.

“These results reinforce our conviction that mRNA has the potential to fundamentally change how we treat cancer,” Stéphane Bancel, CEO of Moderna, said in a statement.

The pharmaceutical companies emphasized that the vaccine is not designed to prevent melanoma but rather to reduce the risk of recurrence and death following prior medical treatment. They also noted that the therapy remains under investigation to validate its efficacy and obtain approval from health authorities before widespread use.

Most people diagnosed with melanoma have a high chance of survival, especially if detected early, but as one of the most common cancers, melanoma kills over 50,000 people globally each year. In theory, similar vaccines could be developed for other kinds of tumors in the future. Earlier this year, another trial demonstrated that personalized mRNA vaccines can greatly improve survival among patients with pancreatic cancer, one of the deadliest kinds of tumor.

“This is the major breakthrough, because conceptually it would make it possible to treat not only melanoma but also other tumors in which the immune system can be reactivated,” Marisol Soengas—a leading melanoma researcher at Spain’s National Cancer Research Center also not involved in the trial—told SMC Spain.

That doesn’t mean a cure for cancer is right around the corner. “The challenge is to ensure that this personalization can be scaled up to a large number of patients and that the cost is affordable for health care systems,” Soengas said. “It is logistically complex. It is literally a bespoke vaccine.”

This story originally appeared on WIRED en Español and has been translated from Spanish.

#627. Scientists discover a hidden switch that burns fat and blocks new fat cells

📅 Oct 24, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Scientists have identified a remarkable protein switch that influences whether cells burn fat or store it. Disabling this protein increases fat consumption and interferes with the formation of new fat cells, raising the possibility of a different approach to treating obesity. Subsequent research has strengthened the discovery, revealing that the same protein helps regulate how much energy the body burns.

What if one of the keys to fighting obesity lies not in suppressing appetite, but in changing how the body's cells use energy?

Researchers at the Weizmann Institute of Science have identified a protein called MTCH2, nicknamed "Mitch," that plays an important role in controlling metabolism. When scientists disabled this protein in laboratory experiments, cells began consuming more fuel, including fats and carbohydrates. They also found that removing Mitch could interfere with the process through which immature cells develop into fat cells.

The discovery builds on earlier experiments in mice, in which switching off Mitch in muscle tissue increased energy expenditure, improved endurance, and protected the animals from diet-related obesity.

The findings, published in The EMBO Journal in January 2025, suggest that manipulating this protein could eventually offer a way to influence both fat burning and fat accumulation. Additional research published later that year has provided further evidence of its importance in regulating body fat and metabolism.

Why Scientists Are Searching for Better Obesity Treatments

Over the past decade, medications designed to promote weight loss have transformed obesity treatment. Drugs such as semaglutide and tirzepatide have helped many patients achieve substantial reductions in body weight, largely by influencing appetite and the body's responses to food.

But losing weight does not necessarily mean losing only fat.

Weight reduction can also involve a decline in lean body mass, which includes muscle and other tissues. Preserving muscle is especially important because it supports strength, mobility, and metabolic health.

A 2026 analysis of 20 clinical trials involving 15,782 participants found that lean mass accounted for approximately 25% to 39% of the weight lost with several widely used weight-loss medications. Importantly, the researchers found that comparable proportional losses also occurred with lifestyle interventions, while adding resistance training helped preserve more lean tissue.

These concerns have encouraged scientists to investigate ways to reduce body fat while protecting the muscles needed for long-term health.

Professor Atan Gross and his colleagues at the Weizmann Institute arrived at a possible avenue through an unexpected discovery involving Mitch.

Switching Off One Protein Protected Mice From Obesity

In earlier experiments, Gross's team reduced the activity of MTCH2 in the muscles of mice. The effects were striking.

The genetically modified animals showed increased metabolic activity and were resistant to obesity caused by their diet. Their muscles also developed more fibers with a high capacity for using oxygen, helping improve physical endurance.

The mice performed better in exercise and stress tests and showed improvements in heart function. In addition to resisting excess fat accumulation, they experienced favorable changes in their overall body composition.

The results suggested that Mitch was doing much more than controlling a single metabolic reaction. Somehow, this protein appeared to influence the body's ability to use energy, store fat, and sustain physical activity.

That raised an intriguing question: How could disabling one protein produce such widespread effects?

To investigate, the researchers turned their attention to mitochondria, the microscopic structures responsible for supplying much of the energy that cells need to function.

The Mitochondria Connection Behind Fat Burning

Mitochondria are frequently described as the powerhouses of cells because they convert energy from food into a usable form called ATP, or adenosine triphosphate.

ATP acts as a cellular energy currency. Muscles use it to contract, cells need it to grow and repair themselves, and countless biological processes depend on a steady supply.

However, mitochondria are not static structures. They constantly change shape, joining together into larger networks or separating into smaller units.

When mitochondria fuse, they can form interconnected systems that help coordinate energy production. When they divide, the resulting structures may operate differently, altering how efficiently cells produce and consume energy.

Gross's team had previously established that Mitch plays a major role in mitochondrial fusion, the process that allows separate mitochondria to merge.

Without this protein, the mitochondrial network becomes fragmented. Although the cells can still generate energy, they experience changes in energy efficiency and demand that can force them to consume more fuel.

This offered a possible explanation for the unusual metabolism observed in the mice. Instead of conserving energy efficiently, their cells appeared to be operating in a more energy-intensive state.

But would disabling Mitch have a similar effect in human cells?

Human Cells Begin Burning More Fat Without Mitch

To answer that question, researchers led by doctoral student Sabita Chourasia used genetic engineering to remove MTCH2 from human cells grown in the laboratory.

The change disrupted the normal structure of their mitochondrial networks. Individual mitochondria became more separated, and the balance between energy production and energy demand shifted.

The cells developed a persistent shortage of readily available energy relative to their needs, even as their mitochondria increased their oxygen-consuming activity.

To compensate, they began consuming more nutrients.

"After deleting Mitch, we examined, every few hours, the effect that had on more than 100 substances taking part in metabolism in human cells," Chourasia explains. "We saw an increase in cellular respiration, the process in which the cell produces energy from nutrients, such as carbohydrates and fats, using oxygen. This explains the increase in muscular endurance in previous experiments using mice."

Cellular respiration is the series of chemical reactions that allows cells to extract usable energy from nutrients. Increasing this activity generally requires a greater supply of fuel.

The researchers found that cells lacking Mitch consumed more fats, carbohydrates, and amino acids, the building blocks of proteins.

Fat metabolism was particularly revealing.

Rather than relying on their usual mix of energy sources, the altered cells increased their use of fatty acids for fuel. This indicated that MTCH2 influences not only how much energy cells consume, but also which nutrients they use to meet that demand.

"We discovered that deleting Mitch led to a major drop in fats in membranes," Gross explains. "At the same time, we saw an increase in fatty substances used to produce energy, and we realized that the fat was being broken down from the membrane to be used as fuel. In other words, we showed that Mitch determines the fate of fat in human cells."

Cell membranes are thin, flexible barriers made largely from fatty molecules called lipids. They protect cells and organize many of their essential functions.

The findings suggested that cells deprived of Mitch were changing how they distributed and used these lipids, drawing on some of their available fatty resources to support increased energy consumption.

Detailed analysis also revealed changes in stored lipids, demonstrating that the effect was more complex than simply eliminating cellular fat. Overall, the results showed that MTCH2 is a powerful regulator of how cells allocate nutrients between energy production and other biological needs.

The researchers then investigated whether Mitch also influences how fat cells develop.

This question was particularly interesting because previous studies had found elevated MTCH2 levels in fat tissue from women with obesity.

Fat cells, also known as adipocytes, do not appear fully formed. They develop from immature precursor cells through a process called adipogenesis, or fat cell differentiation.

As these precursor cells mature, they must build new cellular structures, produce fats, and activate genes that allow them to specialize in storing energy.

All of this requires considerable energy and raw materials.

Gross's team suspected that removing Mitch might disrupt the process by creating an environment in which cells were too busy consuming fuel to build the components needed for fat storage.

Experiments using mouse precursor fat cells supported this idea. When the researchers deleted MTCH2, far fewer cells successfully developed into mature fat cells.

In the published experiments, approximately 80% to 90% of normal precursor cells differentiated within six days, compared with only 5% to 10% of cells lacking MTCH2. The altered cells also accumulated substantially fewer lipid droplets, the structures used to store fat.

"When we deleted Mitch from the progenitor cells, we discovered that the environment created in these cells was not conducive to the synthesis of new fats," Gross explains. "Reducing the ability to synthesize membranes prevents the cells from growing, developing and reaching the point where differentiation is possible. The process of fat accumulation requires a large amount of available energy, but in cells without Mitch, there is a shortage of energy. In addition, the expression of genes necessary for differentiation is suppressed, and there is a shortage of the substances vital for this process to occur. As a result, differentiation of new fat cells is reduced, along with fat accumulation."

In other words, disabling Mitch appeared to affect fat metabolism in two distinct ways. It increased the consumption of energy-rich nutrients and interfered with the cellular machinery needed to develop new fat-storing cells.

Importantly, the fat cell differentiation experiments used a mouse-derived cell line, while the detailed analysis of nutrient consumption was conducted in human cells. The combined results suggest a broader role for the protein, but they do not establish that blocking MTCH2 would prevent new fat cells from forming in people.

Follow-Up Research Reveals Another Fat-Burning Mechanism

The importance of MTCH2 became even clearer in research published on October 3, 2025, in Nature Communications.

An international research team, which included Gross and Chourasia, investigated how MTCH2 influences metabolism in fat tissue. By analyzing human and mouse fat samples, studying cultured fat cells, and conducting experiments in genetically modified mice, the scientists identified another mechanism linking this protein to energy expenditure.

They found that MTCH2 helps regulate the movement of fatty acids into mitochondria, where these molecules can be broken down to produce energy.

The process involves an enzyme called carnitine palmitoyltransferase 1, or CPT1. This enzyme acts as an important checkpoint, helping determine how much fat can enter mitochondria for use as fuel.

Normally, CPT1 activity is regulated by a molecule called malonyl-CoA, which signals when cells should limit fatty acid consumption.

The researchers discovered that MTCH2 physically interacts with CPT1 and influences how strongly this regulatory signal affects the enzyme.

Removing MTCH2 altered that control system, allowing mitochondria to increase fatty acid utilization and energy expenditure.

This provided a more detailed molecular explanation for why disabling the protein could encourage cells to burn additional fat.

Mice Burned More Energy Without Eating Less

The follow-up study also produced an especially promising result.

When researchers removed MTCH2 specifically from the fat cells of mice, the animals burned more energy without increasing their physical activity or changing how much food they consumed.

Under a regular diet, the genetically modified mice gained weight at a rate similar to that of normal mice.

However, when the animals were fed a high-fat diet, a difference emerged.

Mice lacking MTCH2 in their fat tissue gained less weight and accumulated less body fat than untreated mice, despite consuming similar amounts of food.

Their lean body mass remained comparable to that of the control animals.

The genetically modified mice also demonstrated improved glucose tolerance, indicating that their bodies were better able to manage blood sugar after receiving glucose. In addition, their cholesterol levels were lower.

These findings strengthened the evidence that MTCH2 influences whole-body metabolism, rather than simply changing the behavior of isolated cells.

The scientists also found that its metabolic effects could occur independently of UCP1, a protein commonly associated with heat production in brown fat. Brown fat is a specialized form of body fat that helps generate heat by burning energy, whereas white fat primarily stores excess energy.

Discovering an additional pathway for increasing energy expenditure could be important because it offers researchers another potential target for treating obesity and related metabolic disorders.

Could This Protein Lead to Better Weight-Loss Treatments?

Together, the findings suggest that MTCH2 may be an important molecular switch linking fat storage, energy consumption, and the development of fat cells.

The original experiments showed that removing the protein could increase nutrient consumption in human cells and interfere with fat cell formation in mouse cells. The later research demonstrated that selectively removing MTCH2 from fat tissue could increase energy expenditure and protect mice against excessive weight gain on a high-fat diet.

That combination raises the possibility of developing treatments that change how the body handles energy, potentially complementing medications that primarily work by reducing appetite.

However, several major questions remain unanswered.

Scientists have not yet demonstrated that inhibiting MTCH2 produces safe, sustained weight loss in humans. The protein also participates in other important biological activities, including mitochondrial regulation and pathways involved in cell survival. Blocking it throughout the body could therefore produce unwanted consequences.

Preventing fat cells from forming is not necessarily beneficial in every situation, either. Healthy fat tissue plays an essential role in storing excess energy, maintaining blood sugar balance, and producing hormones. Disrupting its function could potentially redirect fats into organs where they may cause harm.

Researchers will need to determine whether MTCH2 can be targeted safely, which tissues would benefit most, and whether reducing its activity would preserve muscle health while promoting fat loss.

For now, established strategies such as resistance exercise and adequate protein intake remain important for protecting muscle during weight reduction. A 2026 review of clinical trials found that combining lifestyle changes with resistance training produced a more favorable pattern of lean mass preservation than weight-loss interventions without such exercise.

The research nevertheless points toward a compelling possibility: Rather than focusing exclusively on how much food the body takes in, future obesity treatments might also influence how cells decide whether to burn energy or store it.

And one protein, Mitch, appears to be deeply involved in that decision.

The original study, titled "MTCH2 controls energy demand and expenditure to fuel anabolism during adipogenesis," was published in The EMBO Journal on January 3, 2025.

The research was led by Sabita Chourasia and Professor Atan Gross at the Weizmann Institute of Science.

Additional contributors included Dr. Christopher Petucci, Clarissa Shoffler, and Dina Abbasian from the University of Pennsylvania in Philadelphia; Hu Wang and Professor Xianlin Han from the University of Texas at San Antonio; Dr. Ehud Sivan, Dr. Alexander Brandis, Tevie Mehlman, Dr. Sergey Malitsky, Dr. Maxim Itkin, Dr. Ayala Sharp, Dr. Ron Rotkopf, and Dr. Bareket Dassa from Weizmann's Life Sciences Core Facilities Department; and Dr. Limor Regev and Dr. Yehudit Zaltsman from Weizmann's Immunology and Regenerative Biology Department.

The follow-up study, "MTCH2 modulates CPT1 activity to regulate lipid metabolism of adipocytes," was published in Nature Communications on October 3, 2025. It involved an international collaboration, including researchers affiliated with ETH Zurich, Weizmann Institute of Science, and other institutions.

Materials provided by Weizmann Institute of Science. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#1687. A sweet end to the week against the Toffees?

📅 Oct 24, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

After Lewis started yesterday’s blog with an ode to Berlin’s crisp autumnal air, I feel it’s my duty to point out that the weather is due to be fucking horrible in London today. There’s a storm on its way, with very heavy rain forecast across the capital from this afternoon until the early hours. If you’re heading to the match, you’ll want to reach for whatever waterproof Adidas merch drop you’ve been tempted into buying in recent years. And an umbrella. And possibly galoshes. Look ’em up, kids.

Weather aside, a showdown with Everton is always welcome, particularly on home turf. I was looking through the record books yesterday, and the Toffees have won just four of their last 50 visits to Highbury and the Emirates in all competitions. Arsenal have won 38, outscoring them 109–36. What’s more, their only win in north London this millennium came during the pandemic, so it doesn’t really count. We’ve faced each other 226 times in competitive fixtures. Plenty of history to get through, most of it rather enjoyable from our perspective.

My own fond memories stretch back to the early nineties, listening to Jonathan Pearce’s commentary on Capital Gold Sportstime. That’s how I experienced Anders Limpar’s four assists for Wrighty in 1991, Wrighty’s lob over Neville Southall the following year, and even Tony Adams’ title-clinching goal in 1997/98. We’ve given Everton a few batterings since then, too, including the 7-0 at Highbury in 2005 and a couple of 5-1 wins at our new home in the last decade.

More of the same would be lovely, although the Premier League rarely dishes out winning margins like that these days. A disciplined David Moyes side who’ve conceded just seven league goals won’t be inclined to hand out gifts. The Toffees have also had a whole week to stew on their 2-0 defeat to Chelsea, while we’ve been to Germany and back, picking up a nasty injury to our star player along the way.

As you might expect, Bukayo Saka was a hot topic at yesterday’s press conference. Arteta sounded pretty pessimistic, despite the winger still needing another MRI scan and further specialist opinions. I know we shouldn’t speculate on such matters (unless we have English Literature degrees), but I’d be surprised if we see him again this year.

It does raise uncomfortable questions about how we’ve managed him. Asked whether Saka is less robust than he used to be, Arteta pointed to the demanding schedule.

“He’s certainly been different in the last year or so, but the demands that go into the players are immense with club and international competitions, and the times between games are getting narrower. There are less than 66 or 67 hours between games, and every hour is important. The support would be really appreciated, and it’s the same for every team.”

He has a valid point. But isn’t it also our responsibility to manage him through that schedule? Did he need to start nine of the 12 games we’ve played this season?

Remember, he went all the way to the final weekend of the World Cup, then played the last half-hour of the Community Shield less than a month later. I know schools of thought differ on these matters. Players want to maintain their rhythm, managers want their best players on the pitch, and so on. But he was missing games with an Achilles problem in April and was still managing it Stateside, where he had to sit out England matches.

Saka remains a phenomenal player. It just feels as though the minutes he played between breaking through as a 17-year-old and the hamstring injury that required surgery in December 2024 may have caught up with him. That’s just my feeling. Understandably, Arteta sees it differently.

Asked if Saka is burnt out, the manager said: “I don’t have that feeling at all. But certainly what we have to be very mindful of is to create the right ecosystem around a player to maximise his qualities. We need to understand what he’s capable of sustaining when he comes back, whenever that is.”

Ever the pragmatist, Arteta has already told Saka that, whatever he’s dealing with, the team still needs his energy around the place.

“I’ve already told him, go in that building and be the first one there with a good spirit and energy because that’s what the team needs. If everybody is like, ‘Bukayo, how are you? Are you sad? Are you OK?’ That’s exactly what we don’t need.

“It is what it is. This is part of our job to have this, to have some nice and bad results, a defeat, a problem; we need to deal with that. We cannot change that now. What we certainly can dominate is the narrative from here to the Everton game, together with the best energy and the spirit to go and win the game again.”

When it comes to who can fill in for Saka, options are thin on the ground. It looks as though Noni Madueke’s brief flirtation with the left flank is over as quickly as it started, or else it’s Max Dowman. You’d expect the former to start today, with the latter getting minutes against Fleetwood Town in the League Cup on Tuesday. Given Max’s dramatic contribution against the Toffees earlier this year, though, you can’t rule out an appearance this afternoon. Running the ball from the halfway line into an empty net is a decent audition.

Elsewhere, Arteta confirmed Piero Hincapie is available after his recent ankle problem, giving him the option to hand Riccardo Calafiori a breather. Ben White could cover for Jurrien Timber, while Myles Lewis-Skelly, Mikel Merino and Viktor Gyokeres will be champing at the bit to start after coming off the bench in Munich. There’s a balance to strike between resting the likes of Rice and Bruno and giving their new partnership time to bed in. I expect some, but not lots of rotation.

Having spent much of this season warming the bench, Martin Zubimendi must be wondering how he gets back into the side. He was our second-most-used outfield player last season, so it’s quite a change in circumstances. Arteta touched on his situation yesterday:

“He had a few [opportunities], then he had the [ankle] injury, and it’s true he had one game where he was almost fully available to play some minutes, but not to start the game. And from now, he needs to earn the right to play. I need to find the right games to give him the opportunity, and I know when I do that, what his response is going to be. But he’s been exceptional, whatever the role that he had to assume. And I expect that from every player. And he’s a player that deserves more minutes at the moment.”

From what I can tell, Everton arrive with a completely clean bill of health, which is pretty remarkable given their squad is so small. After joining from Arsenal with an Injury, Christian Norgaard was fit enough to make his long-awaited debut last weekend. I was watching at home, and to say he looked off the pace would be an understatement. He was comically slow, like he was wearing tree trunks for boots.

Speaking yesterday about the cameo, David Moyes said: “When we brought him on, we realised he’s a little bit further away than we hoped…Christian was the first one to come to me and say he was sorry about it.”

Once today is out of the way, let’s hope he gets up to speed, any kind of speed, because he seems like a nice fella. The Dane isn’t the only reunion on the cards, we’ll also welcome back Ainsley ‘the Bird Catcher’ Maitland-Niles. Honestly (and I know it’s wishful thinking), but if we’re a few goals ahead in the dying seconds, I’d be up for conceding a cheeky penalty just to see what he does.

At this stage of the season, it’s about putting points on the board, even if the usual tension is dampened somewhat by Manchester City’s impending relegation to Hackney Marshes. The league ‘leaders’ face Aston Villa in the early kick-off, but whether they win 10-0 or lose by the same scoreline, they currently exist like a turd that can’t be flushed.

To be fair, given the weather and the state of the UK’s godforsaken water companies, flushing them would probably only ruin some poor swimmer’s day. So maybe we can wait a bit longer.

I hate to segue from such grotesque imagery to the recently departed Mr Friar, but I believe the club will be paying special tribute to our life president this afternoon. There have been plenty of warm words and anecdotes about his incredible service to Arsenal, and it’ll be good for those of us heading to N5 to pay our respects together.

That’s about it from me. As you’ll know by now, Andrew M has been out of action for a couple of days, so the rest of the team will do its best to bring you as much of the usual matchday coverage as possible. I don’t yet know whether that will include a Live Blog or Player Ratings, but remember, the new Arseblog app on iOS and Android has plenty of new features, including live match updates. There’s just less swearing…

Right, I’m off to find my wetsuit. Come on you Gunners!

#4776. America Finally Approved This Sunscreen Ingredient After 27 Years. I Put It to the Test.

📅 Oct 23, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

There’s plenty of buzz around BEMT right now. Does it live up to the hype?

Our product picks are editor-tested, expert-approved. We may earn a commission through links on our site. Why Trust Us?

TAKE ONE LOOK at our healthcare system, and it’s clear the US is still behind other developed nations in many ways. While affordable options, non-apocalyptic wait times, or the ability to easily speak to a human being on the phone when making an appointment are as fantastical in this country as Voldemort's horcruxes or the idea of the New York Jets ever winning the Super Bowl, this year brought a glimmer of progress toward the rest of the world. At least when it comes to sunscreen.

After the FDA approved bemotrizinol (BEMT for short) in June—a popular active in sunscreens used in Asia, Europe, and Australia since the late 90s—brands are finally now able to release products featuring it in the States. One of the first—and the best so far—is La Roche-Posay Anthelios UVDefense Sunscreen SPF 50 with BEMT, which was released early this week.

I was lucky enough to get an early look at the stuff and have been using it every day for over a month. Does it feel different than other SPFs? Is it effectively protective against UV rays? Will the Jets ever field a decent quarterback? Two of those questions I’ll answer below (and hey, who knows, maybe Arch Manning can save the New York somehow still faithful).

Obviously, the big inclusion here is BEMT, the first newly approved sunscreen filter since 1999. BEMT is a chemical UV filter, meaning it absorbs UV rays rather than blocking them as mineral sunscreen filters do. Okay, rad, but that’s nothing new. According to this writer, La Roche-Posay Anthelios Clear Skin Dry Touch SPF 60 is the best sunscreen for men—it’s also chemical-based and nearly half the price. So why all the fuss about BEMT?

Unlike many other products, BEMT absorbs both UVA rays (the ones that cause early signs of aging and can be a major contributor to skin cancer) and UVB rays (the ones that lead to surface-level damage and sunburn). BEMT can also absorb faster than other chemical actives, with a more lightweight application. Plus, it’s highly photostable, meaning it won’t break down and protect less effectively after being exposed to sunlight (a common issue with many SPF products).

La Roche-Posay tosses some classic chemical ingredients into this formula, as well, for good measure. There’s avobenzone, homosalate, octisalate, and octocrylene, which is the same quartet that highlights my go-to Anthelios Clear Skin Dry Touch SPF 60. There are even some skin-boosters in the mix, like glycerin and soybean oil for added hydration. Of course, it wouldn’t be a La Roche-Posay product if it wasn’t safe for sensitive skin, so it’s also fragrance-free.

When I first applied Anthelios UVDefense with BEMT, it was clear this was different from other sunscreens I've used. It has the look and weight of a thicker mineral sunscreen with the milky texture of a daily moisturizer—but absorbs faster than a gel SPF like Supergoop Unseen. We throw around the terms fast-absorbing and weightless a lot in skincare, but this is one of the rare occasions when a product really lives up to this claim.

I’ll start applying a dime-sized drop of this stuff, and it’s fully absorbed onto my face within 15 seconds without a trace. Not sure if this says more about how lightweight the sunscreen is or how useless my brain is before coffee, but there have been a couple of mornings when I couldn’t remember whether or not I applied the SPF—and had no way of determining if I did.

There is no semblance of a white cast after applying this stuff; it really feels and looks more like applying a lightweight moisturizer than SPF. There’s no weird chemically smell either, which is a sunscreen pet peeve of mine. The only major drawbacks are that it’s not water-resistant or sweat-resistant. But that’s what sunscreen for working out is for.

It’s always difficult to fully quantify how effective a sunscreen is at protecting against signs of aging or cancer, as these things develop over many months and years. However, I never saw any sun damage on days I applied this SPF. As a pasty dude, the biggest tell for if a sunscreen is effective for me is how susceptible my skin becomes to redness. Here, there were no signs of flushing or discoloration during the month I was using the sunscreen.

La Roche-Posay Anthelios UVDefense Sunscreen SPF 50 with BEMT takes full advantage of its newly available in the US hero ingredient with a lightweight formula that feels more like a moisturizer than sunscreen. It absorbs in seconds, leaves no white cast or chemical smell, and works especially well for everyday wear. The fragrance-free formula also makes it a strong pick for sensitive skin. The effective sun protection and quick application make a strong case for why we should have had access to BEMT in this country years ago.

Christian is the Style and Reviews Director at Men's Health and Women’s Health, where he oversees shopping and style content for both brands. He started his magazine career as an accessories assistant at Vogue, and has since held editorial roles at Tatler Asia and The Manual. When he’s not online shopping, you can find him on long walks with his two corgis.

#5510. Florida Farmers Are Making The Move To Regenerative Agriculture

📅 Oct 23, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

As the supervisor of dune plantings at our coastal Florida community, I was keenly aware of the rare February 2026 freeze. Plants that generally resist drought, wind, and salt were burnt by the 26°F temperatures. Luckily, most of our east coast plants survived and have returned to robust health after a series of late summer rains.

But many Florida farmers weren’t so lucky. The state has 44,000 farms on 9.7 million acres, which comprise about 30 percent of the state’s total land. As reported by Sara Pepitone on Inside Climate News, Florida farmers experienced estimated losses of $3.2 billion in 2026. Add to that number the impending consequences of an extended drought, increased wildfire activity, and a warming climate; the threats to local agriculture are palatable.

Not all of Florida’s farmers are succumbing to what might seem inevitable, though. Some growers have decided that regenerative agriculture may be at least a partial solution to their pressing dilemmas.

Renew. Repair. Reinvigorate. Restore. Revive. Each of these verbs helps us to understand what regenerative agriculture practices mean.

Soil is not static: its composition is changeable, based on the weather, which organisms constitute it, which plants grow in it, and other variables. It’s a complex living ecosystem teeming with microbes, nutrients, and other organisms. Healthy soil contains everything necessary for plants to thrive.

Healthy soils are the foundation for agricultural development, food security, ecosystem functions, biodiversity, and resilience to climate change. Why is this?

By building organic matter into soils, regenerative agriculture produces stronger yields and nutrient-rich crops. It leads to resiliency — diminishing erosion and runoff, improving water quality on and off the farm, and helping farms to better withstand climate change impacts like flooding and drought.

Protection that improves yields, helps livelihoods, and builds resilience: these are all common results from regenerative agriculture. Strategies like reduced tillage and increased biological diversity help soil retain water, withstand extreme weather and remain productive. Improvements to soil erosion, loss of soil organic matter, and water quality can be a starting place.

Many farmers who make the transition and see regenerative agriculture as a long-term investment strategy for reducing risk are experiencing positive results. For example, Florida Crystals is now farming 10,000 of its 194,500 acres in Palm Beach County regeneratively.

Then again, food systems are embedded in and interact with other systems—such as environmental, economic, and political systems—and are influenced by a range of local, regional, and global actors. The University of Florida’s Institute of Food and Agricultural Sciences public outreach program works with farmers to determine soil type and how to enrich it for optimal health.

Yes, as Pepitone notes, farmers transitioning to regenerative systems face years of experimentation with possible initial weaker crop yields, the need to purchase new equipment, heat or moisture stress on cover crops, non-cash plants that protect soil between cash crop plantings, and other transitional factors.

Lots of farmers who are making the switch to regenerative agriculture also foreground healthy and fair conditions for farmworkers. A new $700 million USDA pilot program encourages practices that improve long-term stability. For the USDA pilot program to have its greatest impact, the voices of farmers, food producers, Indigenous land caretakers, and workers must not be ignored.

Respect for workers is part of a larger picture of collaboration with soil and local ecosystems, acknowledging the power of the environment to affect crop yields.

Carolyn Fortuna, PhD, is a writer, researcher, and educator with a lifelong dedication to ecojustice. Carolyn has won awards from the Anti-Defamation League, The International Literacy Association, and The Leavey Foundation. Carolyn owns a 2022 Tesla Model Y as well as a 2017 Chevy Bolt. #4696. The 5-Second Rule is scientifically false

📅 Oct 23, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Published Oct 23, 2026 4:07 PM EDT

Since elementary school, we’ve all been taught the “5-Second Rule.” The idea is simple: Food dropped on the floor is still perfectly edible if retrieved in under five seconds. The logic behind the theory, such as it is, is that germs and dirt and pet dander are somehow caught by surprise by the sudden arrival of food and need a few seconds to get their bearings. If you snatch the food up quick enough, they won’t have time to get it “dirty.”

Even as kids, we were skeptical but admit it–you still roll the dice on it every now and again. It’s like shouting “shotgun” before getting into the passenger seat even if it’s only you and the driver. It’s instinctual. But is it true?

“Let’s go with ‘No,’ there is no truth to the ‘5-second rule’,” says Gillson Sciences Director of Operations Erin Thane. “But I would argue that specifics matter.”

As Thane explains, variables such as comparative wetness of said food and type of flooring can swing things one way or another. “Food dropped on a carpet will likely pick up less contamination than food dropped on a smooth surface with the same microbial loads. Sticky or wet foods will likely pick up more contamination than dry foods dropped on the same contaminated surfaces.”

And that’s not all. “There are actually a lot of nuances to this question,” says Thane. “Is it the floor of Windsor Castle or a public bathroom? Was sticky gum dropped and then put back in the mouth, or was it a goldfish cracker? Am I a healthy adult eating something off the ground or an immunocompromised individual?”

Perhaps more surprising, according to Thane, is that the floors in your home and floors in public spaces actually don’t vary in cleanliness as much as you’d like to think. As Thane puts it, “We all wear the same shoes–or socks hopefully in the home–across the floors we encounter daily, like the kitchen and bathroom, to other areas or spaces. We are constantly picking up hitchhiking germs and depositing them from one floor to the next.”

Specifically skin-inhabiting bacteria such as Staphylococcus (which puts the “Staph” in “Staph Infection”) and Corynebacterium (which can cause diphtheria) to gut bacteria such as Bacteroides. Shoes, clothing, and bags can also carry Bacillus or mold or yeast that can cause nausea and vomiting.

While there are mitigating factors, it’s safe to say that in terms of grossness, the “5-Second Rule” is pretty high up there.

“I would certainly stay away from any wet, moist or sticky foods which act like a sponge and will immediately cling to any and all dirt, debris and contamination that it comes into contact with,” advises Thane. “However, after everything we have discussed so far, I refer back to the best practice or rule of thumb which is to just not eat any food off the floor.”

#5728. Why the ‘Odds of Mischief Are High’ in These Midterms

📅 Oct 23, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

This issue is preventing our website from loading properly. Please review the following troubleshooting tips or contact us at [email protected].

Analysis: Why the ‘Odds of Mischief Are High’ in These Midterms

Create an FP account to save articles to read later.

Downloadable PDFs are a benefit of an FP subscription.

Contact us at [email protected] to learn about upgrade options, unlocking the ability to gift this article.

Get audio access with any FP subscription.

With U.S. President Donald Trump’s approval rating at record lows amid high inflation spiked by an unpopular war, many pundits view the upcoming midterm elections as the only serious emergency brake left for the nation.

But even if, as expected, Democrats win substantial victories in the House and possibly the Senate—and if Trump doesn’t follow through on his threats to call it fraud and somehow nullify the vote—the nation may still be heading into a political morass.

With U.S. President Donald Trump’s approval rating at record lows amid high inflation spiked by an unpopular war, many pundits view the upcoming midterm elections as the only serious emergency brake left for the nation.

But even if, as expected, Democrats win substantial victories in the House and possibly the Senate—and if Trump doesn’t follow through on his threats to call it fraud and somehow nullify the vote—the nation may still be heading into a political morass.

Rather than rallying around a common direction—other than opposition to Trump—the Democratic Party appears to lack anything like a coherent governing philosophy or compelling leadership. On the contrary, even on the verge of victory on Nov. 3, Democrats seem to be coming apart at the seams ideologically, making a party that is fractious in the best of times much more so today. It is racked by deep fissures between moderates and candidates allied with left-wing populist forces such as the Democratic Socialists of America (DSA), including major divisions over taxes, health care, and Israel.

In a bruising Senate primary race in Michigan, for example, most establishment Democrats backed Rep. Haley Stevens, a moderate. The DSA-aligned candidate, Abdul El-Sayed, a public health official who ran on a progressive platform of Medicare for All and major new taxes on the rich, barely squeaked out a win. That costly battle, along with El-Sayed’s controversial criticism of Israel, has dampened his support among centrist voters in a tightening race against former Republican Rep. Mike Rogers. The state’s former Democratic chair, Lon Johnson, even urged Michiganders not to vote for El-Sayed, saying that doing so would surrender “our party to ideological extremism.”

Democratic Senate candidate Abdul El-Sayed holds a campaign town hall event in Ionia, Michigan, on Oct. 11.Chip Somodevilla/Getty Images

“The Democrats have this incredible capacity to snatch defeat from the jaws of victory,” said Sidney Milkis, a political scientist at the University of Virginia. “I’m worried about voters’ reaction to the Democratic socialist insurgency. Michigan’s a really important race for control of the Senate. El-Sayed is a good candidate, but he has a lot of baggage.”

Many younger voters are openly dismissive of those who, in the absence of any other national figure, remain placeholders at the top of the party: Senate Minority Leader Chuck Schumer and House Minority Leader Hakeem Jeffries. (Schumer’s unfavorability ratings have soared since 2025, and Jeffries’s ratings are only slightly better.)

Much of the party’s direction depends on the size of the Democratic victory, if one occurs, especially in the House. “If it’s narrow, then Jeffries is going to be under pressure from those very far left folks, the DSA, but if it’s large, he can tell them to pound sand,” said Matt Bennett of the Third Way, a centrist Democratic think tank. “But either way, the identity of a party is forged by presidential politics not congressional. What will determine what Democrats are is the presidential primary process.”

That quadrennial beauty contest will get underway early next year. But it is difficult to identify a party savior with a centrist, electable national platform on the order of U.S. Presidents Barack Obama or Bill Clinton—or even Joe Biden.

Much of the charisma and star power grabbing headlines today comes from the far left—with new faces like New York Mayor Zohran Mamdani and others affiliated with the DSA—despite the broad mass of Democrats remaining closer to the center.

That could leave the party vulnerable to a 2028 GOP strategy of tarring Democrats as “communists” or too leftist to govern, possibly costing the election for whoever becomes the party standard-bearer. In the last week, Trump and other Republicans lost no time in painting the violent protests in France as a warning of what could happen in the United States at the hands of left-wing ideologues supporting open immigration and infected by Islamist radicalism.

“When you see burning steeples and raging fires in France, just remember that is what the radical Dumocrats want to do to America,” Trump said in Kansas on Oct. 19. (The French government said the demonstrations have more to do with high prices, especially soaring energy costs, driven up by Trump’s war in Iran.)

Still, most political experts would say there’s a long way to go to 2028. “If you’d asked me after the midterms in 1990, I wouldn’t have known Bill Clinton was coming along to save our party,” Bennett said. “Someone is going to emerge.”

U.S. Sen. Jon Ossoff walks onstage to speak during a midterm election rally in Atlanta, Georgia, United States, on Oct. 17. Erik S. Lesser/AFP via Getty Images

Leading moderate Democratic candidates are clearly vying for a national platform. Among them is Georgia Sen. Jon Ossoff, who has made his Senate race a blunt test of whether the country is willing to endure a “crook president and his crook family,” as Ossoff said at a rally on Oct. 19. “They promised to drain the swamp. Instead, it’s the most corrupt administration of all time, and everybody knows it.”

The fierce divisions within the Democratic Party suggest that even a victorious slate on Nov. 3 would likely end up, once again, defaulting to a least common denominator policy of attacking and investigating Trump.

“The problem is that the party overall enjoys such low levels of approval and trust, even among partisan Democrats themselves, and is characterized by a such a collective quality of leaderless and directionless rule … that the party has little capacity to handle internal disagreements,” said Sam Rosenfeld, a professor at Colgate University and co-author of The Hollow Parties: The Many Pasts and Disordered Present of American Party Politics.

“Democrats have spent a decade reacting to Republican provocations instead of building something worth believing in,” Sarah Longwell, a prominent anti-Trump Republican, wrote in her new book, How to Eat an Elephant: One Voter at a Time. “Ask swing voters what they want from the Democratic Party, and you’ll hear a version of this over and over: Tell us what you’re for.”

Hence next month’s midterm election is largely a battle of negative perceptions that will almost certainly be decided by which party’s reputation is less bad.

“It’s definitely true that the Democrats’ commanding position in the midterms does not reflect much in the way of underlying political strength and popularity,” Rosenfeld said.

There have been attempts to bridge the ideological gaps within the party, but none have taken hold. Few any longer tout an “abundance” agenda popularized last year by the pundit Ezra Klein or so-called supply-side progressivism. The movement to eliminate red tape preventing new infrastructure and construction has lost momentum in the face of public resistance to building data centers.

Another big question is whether Democrats will control the Senate, which remains a close-run affair. “That, to me, is really crucial,” Milkis said. “The Senate has a lot more control over the judiciary and appointments. That would make it much more difficult for Trump to appoint a new [U.S. Defense Secretary Pete] Hegseth.”

Trump speaks at a midterm campaign rally in Dalton, Georgia, on Oct. 16. Brendan Smialowski/AFP via Getty Images

Many experts see the midterm elections as yet another “fulcrum point” for U.S. democracy. That resurrects an overused phrase popularized by Biden during the Trump era.

“This election offers a choice to voters about whether they want constitutional government or not,” said Justin Levitt, an election expert at Loyola Marymount University and former senior policy advisor for democracy and voting rights in the Biden administration.

In recent weeks, Trump’s poll numbers have been trending steadily down. A poll published on Oct. 21 by the New York Times and Siena University showed that 36 percent of likely voters approved of his performance, a new bottom for that survey, while the latest Reuters/Ipsos poll published on Oct. 19 found that Trump’s approval rating has plunged to a record low of 31 percent.

Those numbers have led some Republicans to fear a blowout like the 2006 midterms that gave Democrats both the House and Senate on the back of U.S. President George W. Bush’s deep unpopularity. As Trump has done in Iran, Bush was mired in a highly criticized and costly war of choice in Iraq, and he was dogged by various Republican political scandals and legislative defeats.

This time, a net gain of just four seats in both the House and Senate would do the trick of shifting power, erasing Republicans’ ​218-214 and 53-47 majorities.

Another key question is whether Trump’s plunging numbers—if they precipitate a GOP defeat like what Bush called his “thumping” in 2006—will begin to loosen his decade-long stranglehold on the Republican Party. The president himself has set the stage for his own comeuppance by telling voters to “pretend I’m on the ballot” and relentlessly campaigning across the country. But several establishment Republicans have split from him on Iran and his trade war with Canada. They have also criticized his recent moves to accommodate Russian President Vladimir Putin by waiving sanctions on diesel imports and withdrawing support for Ukraine.

Then there’s the question of whether Trump will even accept the results. “If there’s free and fair election and kind of a transfer of power, it can send a signal to Americans and people around the world that the United States is not destined for a Jan. 6 at every contested election,” said Matthew Dallek, a political scientist at George Washington University, referring to Trump’s attempt to overturn the 2020 election results by calling on his supporters to prevent the certification of Biden’s win (which he failed to do).

“At the same time, there is no single election that can repair the damage to America’s image in the world as a defender of democracy and reliable ally or repair the lost faith of Americans in the ability of government to improve their lives,” Dallek said.

Milkis added: “Right now, I think the country is in the most danger it’s been in since the Civil War, really, given the current polarization.”

Experts are projecting several possible outcomes along a spectrum marked by cautious hopefulness at one end and utter disaster at the other.

The hopeful scenario—call it “back from the brink”—would usher in huge Democratic victories, render Trump a badly weakened and lame-duck president, and perhaps offer a glimmer of a way out of Trumpism and back to constitutionalism.

The other end of the spectrum—call it “into the abyss”—could mean that Trump manages to follow through with plans to conduct massive vote manipulation and fraud, denial of election results, possibly even an attempt to declare a national emergency and send in troops under the Insurrection Act, which permits the use of regular military domestically.

“It seems evident that there are no guardrails erected around him at all,” Bennett said. “The craziest of the crazies are talking to him. Anything is possible, and Trump has proven that, in this term, he’s willing to do basically anything. I think the odds of mischief are high.”

A bicyclist rides past the Supervisor of Elections office, which features a sculptural installation, in Palm Beach, Florida, on Oct. 19.Joe Raedle/Getty Images

But most experts think Trump will not be able to follow through on his worst threats. The courts have already denied his demands to hand over voter rolls, among other setbacks, and his SAVE America Act, which aimed to put new limits on voting, has gone nowhere in Congress. Even the conservative-dominated Supreme Court rejected his efforts to implement new mail-in voting restrictions.

“None of these methods of interference will work,” said Levitt, who has conducted a study on the issue. “The people who run elections are state and local officials, and when he screams about stuff, they’re not going to listen.”

Even if Trump tries to seize ballots, he added, it will be too late: They already will have been counted. Nor is there anything in federal or constitutional law that would allow Trump to declare martial law to prevent the vote.

“The only thing I’m actually afraid of is if we buy the narrative that we the people are not in charge,” Levitt said. “If the environment is so bad that people are scared of the threats and don’t show up at the polls. That’s possible. I don’t think it’s likely.”

Michael Hirsh is a columnist for Foreign Policy. He is the author of two books: Capital Offense: How Washington’s Wise Men Turned America’s Future Over to Wall Street and At War With Ourselves: Why America Is Squandering Its Chance to Build a Better World. X: @michaelphirsh
