#42. Mystery over source of Ebola infection in Nigeria fuels concern about spread of virus

📅 Oct 23, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Switzerland says patient is wife of Swiss diplomat and believed to have contracted virus while travelling in Uganda

Health officials across Africa are racing to determine how Nigeria’s first Ebola patient ⁠in more than a decade ⁠became infected, amid fears ​the outbreak could spread further after reaching a fourth African country.

The Swiss foreign ministry said on Friday the patient in Nigeria, the Ugandan-born wife of a Swiss diplomat based in Abuja, had not travelled to the Democratic Republic of ⁠Congo, where the outbreak was first detected in mid-May before spreading to Uganda, Kenya and now Nigeria. The woman was believed to have contracted Ebola while travelling with her husband in Uganda, the ministry added.

The new information contradicts accounts from Uganda, which previously said the woman had travelled to eastern DR Congo, and will raise concerns about the ability of authorities to contain the outbreak, which is already the most geographically widespread in history.

The Ugandan government reported Ebola cases in May and June before WHO declared it Ebola-free in August. A Ugandan government spokesperson told Reuters on Thursday that Uganda had no ​Ebola cases and that, if ​it did, it would make that information ​public.

The World Health Organization said investigations were continuing to establish the likely source of infection for the Ebola patient in Nigeria.

The Africa Centres for Disease Control and Prevention is investigating whether the patient may have come into contact with an Ebola patient in Kenya, as both travelled from Uganda’s Entebbe airport on 3 October, said Dr Wessam Mankoula, head of emergency preparedness and response at the organisation.

Catherine Smallwood, a WHO Ebola incident manager in DR Congo, told a UN briefing in Geneva via video ‌link on Friday that WHO staff in DR Congo had no information showing the patient in Nigeria had travelled to the country.

Smallwood said a link to the case in Kenya was possible, ​but added that officials would need to compare the timing with when the Nigeria patient’s symptoms began to tell whether the infection more likely happened at the Ugandan airport or elsewhere.

Marion Koopmans, a Dutch virologist and member of the WHO’s Ebola emergency committee, cast doubt on the theory. “I think that is very speculative unless there is evidence of sharing the same ​space in a manner that could explain that,” she said. “Random presence at the same location, a large airport, is not a supported epidemiological link by any standard of what we currently know.”

The Nigerian health minister, Muhammad Ali Pate, said Nigeria’s case remains contained and that contact-tracing was continuing. “We are trying to contain it so it doesn’t go out to the communities. Hopefully, it won’t get to that and we are working hard to ensure that,” Pate said.

Melaku Yirga, vice-president for Africa at Prosper Global (formerly Mercy Corps), said the confirmation of Ebola in Kenya and Nigeria reinforces the need for countries to work closely together to prevent further transmission.

“What matters most now is speed, sharing information across borders without delay and ensuring frontline health workers and local organiaations have the resources to respond quickly,” he said.

Ebola is a highly contagious and often fatal viral disease that affects humans and non-human primates. It spreads through body fluids or contaminated materials and causes organ damage, blood vessel impairment and sometimes severe internal and external bleeding.

The current outbreak is caused by the rare Bundibugyo virus, which has no approved treatment or vaccine. It was first announced on 15 May and has infected more than 9,500 people and killed nearly half of them.

The outbreak is the largest and deadliest in DR Congo’s history and second only ​globally to ​the 2014-16 outbreak in west Africa, which infected ​more than ‌28,600 people ​and ​killed more than 11,000.

Reuters and the Associated Press contributed reporting

#4723. World’s largest wildlife crossing nears completion in California

📅 Oct 23, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Published Oct 23, 2026 11:03 AM EDT

California is known for its giant, multi-laned highways bustling under bright blue skies. And while this infrastructure is useful to humans, the Golden State’s many highways represent an almost impenetrable barrier for wildlife.

Soon, habitats on either side of the 10-laned 101 Freeway in Agoura Hills, California will be reconnected thanks to the world’s largest wildlife crossing, according to the Los Angeles Times. The Wallis Annenberg Wildlife Crossing is scheduled for completion on December 2.

Wildlife crossings are structures that help animals safely cross roads. In the United States, collisions between motorists and large animals take place more than a million times a year. Experts estimate that this results in 400 human deaths every year. But the problem isn’t just road accidents. Habitat fragmentation is also a concern, especially for species that require a large range. Inbreeding can occur if a small enough group of animals becomes isolated.

In the Los Angeles area, mountain lions (Puma concolor), also known as pumas or cougars, are currently the species most vulnerable to genetic isolation caused by roads and development, according to the project’s website. And the 101 Freeway appears to be the most important obstacle, out of all the roads in the area, to the region’s ecological health.

As such, the Wallis Annenberg Wildlife Crossing aims to reconnect local plants and animals in the ecosystem of the Santa Monica Mountains.  It’s about 210 feet long and about 170 to 174 feet wide. A thick, sturdy-looking vegetation-covered bridge already straddles the busy freeway—but the weather might throw a wrench in the project’s schedule.

“We’re just hoping El Niño‘s on our side,” Beth Pratt, president of the Wildlife Crossing Fund, tells the Los Angeles Times, in reference to the climate pattern that typically brings wetter winter weather along the United States’ southern border. Flooding would put work on pause, according to the outlet. “I keep saying, ‘Mother Nature, we’re trying to help you. Let’s not throw any more complications our way.’”

#4568. ADHD Medications May Help Ease Menopausal Brain Fog

📅 Oct 23, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

Medications commonly used to treat attention-deficit/hyperactivity disorder (ADHD) may help reduce certain symptoms of menopausal brain fog, according to a review presented at the 2026 Annual Meeting of the Menopause Society. The findings have not yet been published in a peer-reviewed journal.

The research suggests that both lisdexamfetamine (Vyvanse) and atomoxetine (Strattera) may improve self-reported executive function in perimenopausal and postmenopausal people. However, they showed little to no effect on objective cognitive tests.

While this scoping review showed promising results, experts caution that it may be too early to begin recommending these medications to females experiencing menopause.

Sara Vaughn, MD, Chief Medical Officer at Clair Health, who wasn’t involved in the study, told Healthline that these findings are interesting and hypothesis-generating, but they do not yet establish ADHD medications as a treatment for menopausal brain fog.

Many middle-aged females report experiencing forgetfulness, slow thinking, reduced concentration, and difficulty finding words. This is often referred to as “brain fog,” and is a common occurrence during various stages of menopause.

These symptoms of brain fog also mirror the symptoms of ADHD.

Brain fog may be due to declining or fluctuating levels of estrogen during perimenopause, which affects dopamine and norepinephrine signaling in the prefrontal cortex and the hippocampus. This can impact memory, focus, and executive functioning.

Despite how disruptive these symptoms can be to quality of life, there is currently no medication approved by the Food and Drug Administration (FDA) to manage them. This often leaves clinicians in a position of trying to differentiate between brain fog from menopause and undiagnosed ADHD.

The review analyzed five studies involving females ages 45–60 with their last menstrual period within the past 10 years. The studies all focused on how ADHD medications affect outcome measures of cognition and ADHD severity.

The researchers found that with lisdexamfetamine, scores on the Brown Attention Deficit Disorder Scale (BADDS), a self-report rating scale that measures ADHD symptoms, dropped from 35.7 to 21.2, compared with 29.8 with a placebo. With atomoxetine, BADDS scores decreased from 38.6 to 25.5, compared with 30.1 with a placebo.

There was no significant benefit observed on objective cognitive tests (ex., memory and processing speed).

These findings suggest that certain ADHD medications may improve perceived cognitive symptoms during menopause, although benefits were not demonstrated on objective cognitive tests.

Jennifer Tat, a medical student at the University of Tennessee Health Sciences Center and lead author of the study, noted, “In the population data, symptom severity, not a formal ADHD diagnosis, predicted menopausal burden. Many women were never diagnosed, so relying on a chart diagnosis misses the women most at risk.”

“We have evidence that the menopause transition is associated with measurable brain changes,” said Vaughn. “But no specific brain signature of menopausal brain fog has been established. To study it rigorously, we first need to define what the term means and measure it consistently.”

Limitations of this review include the small number of studies and the small sample sizes. Most of the measures were also self-reported, which leaves room for error.

Also, the FDA has not approved these types of medications for menopausal brain fog, meaning using them for these purposes would be off-label.

“ADHD medications are not without risks, and I think it is too early to use them routinely for menopausal ‘brain fog,’” said Vaughn.

ADHD medications can carry risks, including cardiovascular side effects. Stimulants such as lisdexamfetamine also have the potential for misuse and dependence. These medications should only be used with medical supervision.

“I am a strong proponent of treating the underlying disorder rather than treating isolated symptoms. ADHD and menopausal cognitive symptoms may look alike, but that does not mean they share the same underlying mechanism—or require the same treatment,” noted Vaughn.

Further research, including additional long-term prospective studies, is needed before clinical guidance can be changed

“We hope this opens up the conversations with patients and providers. We recommend clinicians to screen patients for ADHD and, for those who screen positive, refer them to our psychiatric colleagues who have the knowledge and resources to perform a diagnostic test so that those women who have gone undiagnosed with ADHD can get the treatment they need and deserve,” said Tat.

According to the National Council on Aging (NCOA), your brain requires exercise to function optimally, just like your muscles do.

Ways to help keep your brain sharp and manage menopausal brain fog include:

The NCOA also suggests keeping your brain well-exercised by:

You can also speak with your healthcare professional about how menopause, estrogen levels, hormone therapy, and modifiable risk factors like a sedentary lifestyle or high blood pressure can affect cognitive health.

“I want clinicians to know that they must take brain fog seriously. Ask whether the symptoms are new or a lifelong pattern that menopause has worsened. ADHD in women often goes unrecognized for decades,” said Tat.

#675. Scientists discover a hidden messaging system deep inside the brain

📅 Oct 23, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Deep within the brainstem is a tiny group of nerve cells that plays an outsized role in how the brain functions. This region, called the locus coeruleus (LC), gets its name from the Latin words for "blue place." Although it contains only a small fraction of the brain's neurons, it produces norepinephrine (NE), a chemical messenger that helps regulate attention, learning, heart rate, and the body's reactions to stress and excitement.

Scientists have long assumed that the LC operates like a loudspeaker, sending essentially the same norepinephrine signal across the brain. However, a new study published in Nature challenges that understanding. Using brain imaging, recordings of neural activity, genetic techniques, and behavioral experiments in mice, researchers at the Allen Institute discovered that the LC communicates through several distinct pathways. Rather than broadcasting one general message, it delivers signals to specific destinations, with different groups of neurons responding to different experiences.

A Hidden Brain Network That Sends Precise Messages

By tracing the connections of LC neurons, researchers found that different groups of cells communicate with specific regions of the nervous system. These pathways determine where signals trigger the release of norepinephrine, creating a much more organized communication network than previously recognized.

Neurons in the upper (dorsal) portion of the LC primarily connect to the cerebral cortex and forebrain. Meanwhile, cells in the lower (ventral) portion send their signals mainly to the brainstem and spinal cord. This arrangement allows different parts of the LC to influence separate brain functions.

"The findings suggest the brain's norepinephrine system is far more like a targeted postal network than a foghorn," said Karel Svoboda, director of Neural Dynamics at the Allen Institute and study co-author.

Why the Discovery Could Matter for Brain Disorders

Understanding how this signaling network is organized could eventually change how certain neurological and mental health conditions are treated.

Norepinephrine plays a central role in regulating attention and mood, making it an important target for medications used to treat depression, anxiety, and ADHD. Neurons in the LC are also among the earliest brain cells to deteriorate in Alzheimer's disease, suggesting that the new findings could have implications for understanding and treating these conditions.

Current treatments that influence norepinephrine can affect multiple brain circuits at once, sometimes producing beneficial effects in one region while causing unwanted effects elsewhere. A clearer map of the LC's specialized pathways could help scientists develop therapies that act more selectively on the circuits involved in particular symptoms.

The research received support from the National Institutes of Health's Brain Research Through Advancing Innovative Neurotechnologies® Initiative, also known as The BRAIN Initiative®.

Scientists Identify Brain Cells Involved in Learning

The researchers also uncovered an important distinction between the functions of different LC neurons.

Cells in the dorsal LC that connect to the cortex were particularly active during learning. When mice performed decision-making tasks and adjusted their choices after an unfavorable result, these neurons increased their activity.

Neurons in the ventral LC behaved differently. Their activity rose shortly before mice ignored signals indicating that a reward might be available. This suggests that these cells help regulate whether animals respond to opportunities in their surroundings.

"What emerged was a clear map: neurons in the dorsal LC that send signals upward to the cortex are involved in learning, whereas neurons in the ventral region projecting downward to the brainstem and spinal cord govern whether animals engage with their environment at all," said Svoboda. "We also showed that these anatomical differences are mirrored by distinct gene expression patterns."

These findings indicate that the differences between the two groups extend beyond their locations and connections. They also have distinct patterns of gene activity that reflect their specialized roles.

Researchers Discover a Record-Breaking Neuron

The study involved an extensive examination of the mouse brain. Scientists used whole-brain imaging to study nearly 35,000 neurons, analyzed the genetic profiles of almost 400,000 cells, and reconstructed the complete structures of several exceptionally complex nerve cells.

During this work, they encountered another remarkable finding: LC neurons possess extraordinarily long axons. These projections carry signals from nerve cells to other cells, allowing information to travel across the nervous system.

The researchers found that LC axons measured approximately 35 centimeters long on average. One particularly extraordinary axon had a total reconstructed length of 70.32 centimeters, making it part of the longest single neuron ever measured in a mouse.

"This neuron, like many others that we studied, supplies NE to a very large volume of the cerebral cortex. For the brain, this is highly unusual. Most neurons are more specific in their targets," said Jeremiah Cohen, scientist at the Allen Institute and study co-author. "But this neuron doesn't release NE everywhere. It ignores the cerebellum, brainstem, and spinal cord."

Despite its remarkable length and extensive reach, the neuron does not distribute norepinephrine throughout the entire brain. Instead, its connections remain selective, reinforcing the discovery that the LC operates through carefully organized signaling pathways.

A New Understanding of How the Brain Learns

The researchers also identified intriguing similarities between the norepinephrine system and the brain's dopamine signaling system.

Dopamine is another major chemical messenger that sends signals to the basal ganglia, a brain region involved in forming habits. Scientists suspect that dopamine and norepinephrine work together to support learning, potentially allowing the brain to acquire several complex and abstract concepts at the same time.

By revealing the intricate connections, specialized functions, and unusual physical structure of neurons in the locus coeruleus, the research provides a more detailed picture of how the animal brain communicates and learns.

Although the findings come from experiments in mice, they could eventually help scientists better understand human brain function and design treatments that target specific neural circuits rather than affecting entire signaling systems.

Materials provided by Allen Institute. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#724. 40 years later, Chernobyl's nuclear fallout is not what scientists expected

📅 Oct 23, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Four decades after the Chernobyl (known today as Chornobyl) nuclear disaster, microscopic radioactive particles remain scattered throughout the surrounding soil. The explosions that devastated the reactor released enormous quantities of debris, leaving behind contamination that continues to pose serious health risks. Even after 40 years, particles measuring just 8 to 50 micrometers remain highly radioactive, and entering affected areas still requires protective clothing.

Scientists have now taken a closer look at these tiny remnants and uncovered a surprising finding: some have preserved much of their original internal structure despite decades of exposure to the environment.

"There are three classes of these particles," explains Tobias Weissenborn, a physicist and doctoral candidate at Leibniz University Hannover. "First, there are particles that are chemically and physically still very similar to the nuclear fuel uranium dioxide. Then, there are particles that are partially or fully encased in, or completely fused with, their zirconium layer."

The second category originated during the extreme temperatures reached inside the reactor. As the nuclear fuel melted, it combined with zirconium, a highly resistant metal used in the fuel's protective outer layer.

"The third type of particle was created when the graphite-moderator caught fire," Weissenborn adds.

Under normal operating conditions, the graphite moderator slows fast neutrons, helping sustain the nuclear chain reaction. During the disaster, however, the graphite ignited and continued burning for ten days.

"In the process, the fuel oxidized into various uranium oxides" - such as U3O8.

Unlike more stable materials, this uranium oxide is mechanically fragile and can readily break apart into microscopic fragments. Wind can then carry these radioactive particles through the environment, where inhaling them presents serious health hazards.

Scientists Examine Chornobyl's Radioactive Dust in Unprecedented Detail

Despite decades of research, scientists still do not fully understand why radioactive particles from Chornobyl break down at different speeds. As these materials weather, they can produce several forms of uranium oxide, making their long term behavior difficult to predict.

Investigating these changes requires exceptionally precise equipment, including synchrotron X-ray diffraction, a technique that reveals the arrangement of atoms inside crystalline materials.

Dr. Christoph Hennig, a crystallographer at the Helmholtz-Zentrum Dresden-Rossendorf (HZDR), has now completed the first successful structural analysis of radioactive fragments of this kind at the Rossendorf Beamline in Grenoble.

Working with Weissenborn, Hennig analyzed six particles recovered from the Chornobyl region. By identifying the different crystalline phases within them, the researchers gained information that could help determine how quickly radioactive substances escape into the surrounding environment.

"The colleagues from Hanover and I used various methods to isolate the particles from Ukrainian soil samples and attach them to tungsten electrodes," Weissenborn explains.

Because the samples were highly radioactive, they had to be enclosed within several layers of containment before being transported to Grenoble. There, Hennig's research team used X-ray diffraction to examine their internal structures by measuring how X-rays scattered as they passed through the material.

"First, we had to figure out how to examine the particle structure," Christoph Hennig explains. "Ultimately, we fully rotated the particles in an X-ray beam focused down to 100 micrometers - about the thickness of a human hair. We measured each particle from 2,000 different angles to seamlessly capture all the reflections."

The measurements allowed the scientists to distinguish different uranium oxide phases within the radioactive fragments, revealing details that had previously been inaccessible.

A Surprising Discovery About 40-Year-Old Nuclear Fallout

The results revealed something unexpected. Despite spending four decades in the environment, the nuclear fuel inside the six examined particles had retained much of its original crystal structure.

This suggests that at least some of the radioactive debris produced by the Chornobyl disaster is more chemically stable than researchers previously believed.

That stability could have important environmental implications. Rather than rapidly releasing radioactive substances, the particles appear capable of retaining fission products within their structures. If this behavior occurs more widely, it could help limit the movement of radioactive contaminants into nearby soil and water.

However, the researchers emphasize that their findings should not be interpreted as evidence that Chornobyl's radioactive contamination is becoming harmless.

"However, every single particle has a different structure," warns Weissenborn, "and our experiment only studied six such particles from two different locations."

The relatively small sample means the findings cannot yet establish how radioactive particles behave throughout the entire contaminated region. To understand their stability more comprehensively, scientists would need to investigate many additional particles collected from a much broader range of locations.

Why Chornobyl's Radioactive Legacy Remains Unpredictable

Even with substantially more data, predicting the long term dangers of Chornobyl's radioactive debris would remain challenging. Individual particles can differ in composition, structure, and resistance to environmental weathering, potentially affecting when they release radioactive materials.

"And even if we obtained some averages at some point, we still wouldn't be able to make universal statements about health risks in the region," Weissenborn cautions. "Because even if the particles decay in a largely uniform patten, there will always be outliers, more persistent particles, that will release radionuclides at a later point in time."

These persistent particles could continue releasing radioactive substances long after other remnants have broken down, complicating efforts to assess future contamination and health risks.

For that reason, the new findings do not suggest that restrictions on the Chornobyl Exclusion Zone can be removed anytime soon.

Research into the disaster's enduring consequences is continuing. Weissenborn and Hennig are already conducting additional experiments focused on highly radioactive transuranic phases found in the remaining debris. Their work could provide a clearer picture of how Chornobyl's nuclear materials change over time and how long their environmental effects may persist.

#646. Cambridge scientists create a $10 chip that could put a medical lab on your wrist

📅 Oct 23, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

A new optical sensor costing just $10 could bring laboratory-level precision to wearable health monitors, potentially allowing people to track blood glucose without needles. The technology could also transform food testing, industrial quality control, and environmental monitoring.

Researchers at the University of Cambridge and GlitterinTech (a start-up founded by the same research group) have developed a new type of optical spectrometer that combines exceptional accuracy with a remarkably small size and low cost. Measuring just centimeters across, the device could be incorporated into portable electronics and wearable health technology. Its potential applications range from monitoring important health markers in real time to identifying materials and detecting changes in chemical composition.

Optical spectrometers work by analyzing light to reveal information about the substances it interacts with. They are essential tools in medicine, manufacturing, chemical testing, and environmental research. However, making these instruments smaller has traditionally meant compromising their performance. Compact spectrometers often have limited measurement ranges, reduced resolution, or lower accuracy, making them better suited to identifying substances than performing precise measurements.

The researchers believe their new design could overcome these limitations. Known as a convolutional spectrometer, it uses a mathematical principle called the convolution theorem to analyze light in a fundamentally different way. According to the team, this approach delivers performance that surpasses existing types of spectrometers in several key areas, including designs based on light dispersion, Fourier transforms, and computational reconstruction.

A New Way to Analyze Light With Extraordinary Precision

Traditional spectrometers generally separate incoming light into its component wavelengths or use complex calculations to reconstruct its spectrum. The new device takes another approach by performing a mathematical operation called convolution directly on the incoming light.

To accomplish this, the researchers arranged optical components with repeating spectral responses in a sequence. These components can include unbalanced Mach-Zehnder interferometers or micro-ring resonators. By adjusting them in proportion to one another, the system can shift its response to different wavelengths in a controlled, linear fashion. Fast Fourier transforms are then used to accurately recover the light spectrum.

"The key insight was to go back to the mathematics and ask whether there was a fundamentally cleaner way to retrieve spectra," said Dr. Chunhui Yao, a lead author of the study. "By using the convolution theorem directly in the optical domain, we avoid many of the limitations that have held miniaturized spectrometers back. This gives us high precision, strong noise tolerance, and very low computational overhead, all in a compact and low-cost system."

The researchers built the spectrometer using a silicon nitride photonic platform and integrated it with onboard electronics. It operates across a broad range of near-infrared wavelengths (1200-1700 nm) and can collect and process measurements in less than a second.

Another important advantage is its ability to expand its measurement capabilities without major hardware changes. Because the system uses repeating optical patterns, its spectral bandwidth can theoretically be extended almost without limit. Adding more optical components in sequence can also increase its resolution exponentially.

A $10 Sensor That Could Transform Food Testing and Healthcare

To evaluate the device outside purely theoretical measurements, the researchers tested it on a variety of materials and substances. The results demonstrated its potential for applications that normally depend on larger, more expensive laboratory instruments.

When used to identify plastics, pharmaceuticals, coffee, flour, and tea, the spectrometer achieved a 100% classification success rate in the reported tests. It also measured concentrations in water-based and organic solutions with accuracies of approximately 0.01%, exceeding the performance of commercial benchtop spectrometers in those experiments.

The healthcare demonstrations were particularly promising. Under conditions designed to reflect real physiological environments, the device accurately measured several human biomarkers, including skin moisture, blood alcohol, blood lactate, and blood glucose.

The researchers also demonstrated noninvasive glucose tracking over an extended period in one participant. Although further testing would be needed before the technology could become a practical medical product, the finding suggests a possible route toward monitoring blood sugar without repeatedly piercing the skin.

"These biomedical demonstrations are particularly exciting," said Prof. Qixiang Cheng, who led the project. "What makes this work stand out is not just performance in the lab, but technical readiness. Dr. Chunhui Yao's contribution was crucial in translating a mathematical concept into a fully packaged, robust system that operates reliably across temperature extremes and real-world conditions. That combination is what opens the door to practical deployment."

The sensor also demonstrated impressive resistance to temperature changes. It continued operating reliably between -20°C and 80°C, an unusually wide temperature range for a miniature spectrometer. This durability could be especially valuable for wearable devices, factory equipment, and sensors operating outdoors.

Why a Simpler Design Could Be a Major Breakthrough

The new spectrometer's advantages extend beyond its accuracy, size, and price. Its internal design also reduces the amount of computing power needed to analyze incoming light.

Many existing miniature spectrometers depend on complicated calibration procedures and demanding algorithms to reconstruct spectral information. These methods can become unreliable when measurements contain noise, and they often require substantial processing resources.

The Cambridge team's convolution-based approach avoids much of this complexity. Because the measurements follow a linear mathematical relationship, the system can recover spectra rapidly and consistently while using relatively little computational power.

"This is a beautiful example of how elegant engineering can unlock real impact," said Prof. Richard Penty, who contributed to the photonic system design and integration. "The architecture is simple, scalable, and manufacturable, yet it delivers precision that rivals or exceeds much larger instruments. Dr. Chunhui Yao played a central role in bringing together the photonic design, system integration, and experimental validation that made this possible."

Its relatively simple construction could also make the technology easier to manufacture on a large scale, potentially bringing sophisticated optical measurements to devices where conventional spectrometers would be too bulky or expensive.

Bringing Laboratory-Grade Measurements to Everyday Devices

The researchers envision a future in which accurate optical sensing becomes a routine feature of everyday technology rather than something largely confined to specialized laboratories.

In industrial settings, inexpensive spectrometers could be installed throughout manufacturing facilities to continuously check material composition and product quality. Similar sensors could monitor food freshness, identify substances, and analyze environmental conditions on a much larger scale than is currently practical.

Healthcare could see equally significant changes. Compact sensors might eventually enable wearables to measure hydration levels, detect signs of alcohol intoxication, track biomarkers related to physical fitness, or continuously monitor glucose levels for people managing diabetes.

"Our vision is to make spectrometry as ubiquitous as temperature or motion sensing," added Prof. Qixiang Cheng. "This work shows that high-quality spectral information doesn't have to be confined to the laboratory -- it can be embedded directly into the technologies people use every day."

As photonic technology becomes smaller and more advanced, the convolutional spectrometer provides a promising example of how mathematical insights and practical engineering can make sophisticated scientific instruments simpler, cheaper, and more accessible. By combining high precision with a device that costs around $10, the researchers have opened a potential path toward a new generation of smart sensors capable of measuring the hidden chemistry of our bodies and surroundings.

Materials provided by University of Cambridge. Original written by Michael Shuff. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#4286. Scientists discover a hidden immune weapon against deadly lung cancer

📅 Oct 23, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Scientists have identified a previously overlooked type of immune cell that can detect and destroy small cell lung cancer, the most aggressive form of lung cancer worldwide.

The discovery, reported in the journal Cancer Cell by researchers at WEHI, revealed that patients with greater numbers of these specialized immune cells tended to survive longer. The findings point to a previously unrecognized immune defense that could provide a new way to attack tumors that are notoriously difficult to treat.

The research team also demonstrated that medications already in use could enhance the ability of these immune cells to kill cancer. This approach could eventually improve existing treatments and increase the number of patients who benefit from immunotherapy.

Why Small Cell Lung Cancer Is So Difficult to Treat

Small cell lung cancer (SCLC) is among the deadliest cancers, with fewer than 7% of patients surviving for five years after diagnosis.

Immunotherapy has dramatically improved treatment for several types of cancer, but its success against SCLC has been much more limited. Only a small proportion of patients experience lasting benefits.

A major challenge is the cancer's ability to escape immune surveillance. SCLC tumors frequently avoid detection by the conventional immune cells that many immunotherapy treatments depend on, allowing the disease to persist despite the body's natural defenses.

The findings published in Cancer Cell suggest there may be another way for the immune system to recognize and attack these tumors. By identifying a previously overlooked immune pathway, researchers have uncovered a potential strategy for overcoming the cancer's ability to remain hidden.

Professor Kate Sutherland, the study's senior author and a laboratory head at WEHI, said the discovery could help address a longstanding challenge in lung cancer research: finding ways to make immunotherapy effective for more patients.

"For decades, we've known that small cell lung cancer is exceptionally good at hiding from the immune system," Prof Sutherland said.

"We've found an immune cell that appears to be able to see through that disguise.

"Strikingly, patients whose tumors contained more of these specialized immune cells often had better treatment outcomes.

"This is the strongest evidence yet that gamma delta T cells are present in small cell lung cancers and appear capable of fighting the disease.

"It suggests these cells may offer an alternative immune attack route for tumors that are able to evade conventional cancer-fighting immune cells, opening up new possibilities for treating a cancer that has seen few advances over the past decades."

Miniature Tumors Reveal a Powerful Immune Response

To investigate whether gamma delta T cells could directly attack lung cancer, the scientists developed miniature tumors in the laboratory using tissue samples donated by patients. The samples were obtained through the Victorian Cancer Biobank in partnership with the Royal Melbourne Hospital.

These miniature tumors, often called "mini tumors," provide one of the most realistic laboratory models of SCLC created so far, allowing researchers to study how cancer behaves and responds to potential treatments.

The team then introduced gamma delta T cells collected from the same patients' blood into the tumor models. The results showed that these immune cells were highly effective at identifying and destroying SCLC cells.

The scientists went a step further by testing whether two medications already approved for other uses could make this immune response even stronger. The drugs were tarlatamab (a cancer treatment approved for SCLC) and zoledronate (approved for osteoporosis and cancer-related bone disease).

Each medication contributed in a different way. Zoledronate increased the number of available gamma delta T cells and improved their ability to identify the lung cancer tumors. Tarlatamab helped bring the immune cells and cancer cells together, effectively directing the immune attack toward the tumors.

When used together, the drugs substantially increased the ability of gamma delta T cells to kill cancer cells in laboratory experiments. The findings suggest that combining existing treatments could provide a new strategy for strengthening the immune system's response to SCLC.

Could Existing Drugs Lead to New Lung Cancer Therapies?

Dr. Jin Ng, the study's first author, said the discovery could also support a treatment approach known as adoptive cell transfer. This involves collecting immune cells, multiplying them outside the body, and then delivering large numbers of those cells back into a patient to help fight cancer.

Because methods for expanding and reinfusing gamma delta T cells have already been developed, the researchers believe their findings could help accelerate the development of new treatments.

"For patients with lower gamma delta T cell levels, therapies such as zoledronate may offer a way to boost their cancer-fighting immune cells and responsiveness to treatments," Dr. Ng said.

"As therapies that expand and reinfuse gamma delta T cells already exist, our findings raise the exciting possibility of rapidly translating this discovery into new treatment strategies.

"This could open an entirely new avenue for personalized treatment approaches in small cell lung cancer and other solid cancers that are often missed by conventional immune cells."

Clues From Major Lung Cancer Immunotherapy Trials

The investigation began with an examination of data from two influential clinical trials, IMpower133 and CASPIAN. Both studies helped establish immunotherapy as a standard treatment option for patients with small cell lung cancer.

Researchers noticed evidence pointing toward an important role for gamma delta T cells and used sophisticated genomic and computational techniques to investigate further.

Analyses conducted by WEHI's genomics and bioinformatics teams revealed that patients with stronger gamma delta T cell signatures were among those who benefited most from immunotherapy.

Together with the laboratory findings, these results strengthen the evidence that gamma delta T cells play an important role in the immune response to SCLC and could become useful targets for future therapies.

The WEHI team is now working with scientists at the Doherty Institute to build on the discovery, with the goal of translating the findings into a new class of immunotherapy drugs for small cell lung cancer.

The research received support from Cancer Council Victoria, the National Health and Medical Research Council (NHMRC), The CASS Foundation, and The International Association for the Study of Lung Cancer.

Materials provided by Walter and Eliza Hall Institute. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#4937. Not All Runners Feel Welcome on the Trails. These Groups Are Getting Everyone Outside.

📅 Oct 22, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Runners are moving to the trails—and making space for people from all backgrounds to be included.

Welcome to the Wild World of Trail Running! We're diving deep on the races, training tips, and gear you need to know to get outside off paved roads to run a whole new way. Click here for more stories about the trail explosion.

ANTOINE HAUGABOOK, 46, has been immersed in the road running community for more than a decade. A Nashville-based account manager in information technology, Haugabook has served as the co-captain of the city's local chapter of Black Men Run since its inception in 2013 and has also formed relationships with other clubs throughout the region. But when Haugabook and his wife, Yolanda "Yo" Lopez Haugabook, ventured off the roads they were familiar with and into the local trail running scene, they felt like they didn't belong.

"We immediately noticed we were the only people of color and didn't feel like we really related to anyone else out on the trails," Lopez Haugabook, 41, says. "On our third Saturday trail run, one of the other runners finally acknowledged us, saying 'You're back,’ and followed it up with '...Y'all ain't had enough yet?'"

The couple had hit the trails to try something different. After feeling burned out with road running, Antoine applied to and was accepted to the Running Industry Diversity Coalition's 2025 Freedom to Run: Back Outside program, which was designed to get runners of diverse backgrounds to explore the trails. The RIDC program was launched alongside a downloadable resource titled "Opening the Path to Trail Running: A Toolkit for Runners, Retailers, and Communities," which provides BIPOC runners with useful information and resources that act as a welcoming and approachable introduction to trail running. The Haugabooks, kitted out with the gear and coaching provided by RIDC, ventured out on the trails, only to be met with that disconcerting reception.

These types of programs are unfortunately necessary because it’s not an uncommon experience for many people of color when they try to get outside. Nearly 69 percent of outdoor participants are white, according to The Outdoor Industry Association’s 2024 report. Not feeling a sense of belonging is one of the biggest factors that can deter minority runners from exploring trails—but it’s not the only one. Runners who spoke to MH also shared concerns about safety, discouraging finish time cutoffs, and financial constraints, such as high entry fees.

That first experience felt very personal to the Haugabooks. Having long been acquainted with the message that running was for everyone, that microaggression messaged that not everyone was willing to include people who looked like them in the community and reinforced stereotypes that people of color fear and don't enjoy the outdoors.

Those early moments were uncomfortable—but the pair weren’t detered from staying on trail. That just served as fuel to their fire and led them to start Sin Miedo Trail Runners, a group for people of color that currently has around 60 members—most of whom are at the beginner level and experiencing their first introduction to trail running and racing.

"'Sin miedo' literally translates to 'without fear,' but our message is intended more as 'live fearlessly,' because fear tends to take over a lot of BIPOC experiences, whether it's the fear of being persecuted or excluded, and not just in running," Lopez Haugabook says. "'Sin miedo' was a catalyst saying we're going to show up and we will not be afraid to take up space, we will not be afraid to experience the outdoors, even though, culturally, we've been told to be afraid of being in the woods by ourselves, and rightfully so."

Witnessing this lack of representation firsthand prompted Detroit-area content creator Tommie Bailey, a.k.a. "Tommie Runz," 42, to create his "Black Skin Looks Good on Trail" campaign. Bailey has a long list of trail bonafides: he took on the 340 mile Speed Project course from Los Angeles to Las Vegas solo in 2025, he ran this year's Western States Endurance Run 100-miler, and has been a presence at the prestigious Ultra Trail du Mont Blanc (UTMB) series in France over the last two years with major brands.

"I've heard from so many Black and Brown runners over the years saying that they've been inspired to get out on the trails after seeing the content I've put out over the years, and I feel blessed that I've always felt like I've had the freedom to talk about these topics even when collaborating with brands at these events," Bailey says. "We aren't being highlighted to the same degree because there just aren't a lot of Black Americans at the top of roads or trails because it's still new to a lot of us, but I think we need to have the conversations about why Black and Brown people don't feel like they're being represented at these races."

THE CONCEPT OF creating their own spaces isn't new for many minority community leaders, especially as the running industry and corporate America as a whole have shifted away from commitments made during the 2020 social justice reckoning that came after the tragic murders of Ahmaud Arbery and George Floyd. Large brands, including Google, Meta, and Target, have cut DEI programs and positions meant to support employees from marginalized groups, and running community leaders have had to work even harder to highlight instances of racial injustice and accessibility and remind the industry and community that problems weren't solved in 2020.

In 2025, Alison Mariella Désir, co-founder of the RIDC and author of Running While Black: Finding Freedom in a Sport That Wasn't Built for Us, created the We Out Here Trail Festival, which offers race distances from 1.9 miles to two hours, in her home base of Seattle, along with her friend and local running coach and personal trainer Aaliyah Earvin.

"We chose these distance options because most trail races tend to be focused on longer/ultra distances and have discouraging finish time cutoffs that may make newer trail runners hesitant to sign up," Désir, 41, says. "We also wanted distances that people could not compare to road distances so that there were no expectations around what it should feel like, and so they could fully embrace trail running apart from their experiences in road running."

Having grown tired of calling out prominent trail racing leadership for their lack of concrete efforts to diversify their fields and create welcoming communities, she was inspired to create her own event where minority runners could show up as their true selves.

"We Out Here Trail Fest centers the experience of people of color, but white people are there too; we just created it with us in mind," Désir says. "If you can welcome people who are new to the trail and give them this incredible experience, and then they go off to other spaces, but they have this fundamental understanding that they belong, which is a huge service that we can do for the community. I want people to leave with a feeling that trail running is joyful and that it's for them."

Sabrina Pace-Humphreys, 48, a Cotswolds, U.K.-based ultrarunner, similarly used her experiences to create her own community. In 2019, while training for a race in the Italian Alps, Pace-Humphreys was traversing a narrow ridge on a mountain range and had a near-fatal experience when she slipped off the path and was hanging on to the side of the mountain. She recalls being ignored by five men who ran past her as she screamed for help.

"It was kind of a sliding doors moment because I had asked for help and hadn't been given it, and I very much felt as though had I been blonde-haired, blue-eyed, and white, I would have got help a lot quicker," she says. "This incident made me realize that I had to make a decision, which was to either leave trail running because I cannot continue to be in spaces where I feel physically unsafe and uncared for and unseen, or I start something of my own."

This led Pace-Humphreys to create Black Trail Runners, a global community and nonprofit that uses trail running to connect BIPOC runners and their allies with the outdoors. The organization hosts events like Black to the Trails, which organizers call Europe's most ethnically diverse trail running event, with 1K, 5K, and 10K races.

"At the time, there were a lot of running group crews in the U.K., but none that specialized in trail, and none that centered the Black experience in trail running," she says. "With Black Trail Runners, we qualify trail leaders and trail coaches while also addressing barriers to access, skills and representation, both out on the trails and through the media."

DÉSIR ALSO HOSTS the Earth Movers podcast with professional ultramarathoner and 2021 USATF 100-mile national champion Stefanie Flippin and Verna NezBegay Volker, a fellow activist and founder of Native Women Run, a nonprofit dedicated to representing and empowering Native women across the U.S. and Canada. Now in its third season, the three women created the podcast with the goal to amplify the stories and people of color on the trails and beyond, many of whom have not had the opportunity to do so in a corner of the sport that frequently uplifts the same familiar, homogenous voices.

"Trail running is very white-forward right now and it continues to center the experiences of those who are benefiting the most," Flippin, 37, says. "For example, there's the current narrative that women are showing up so much more in trail running, which is incredible, but it's still equally important to highlight the experiences of those who are less represented and things like their safety and other barriers to access that they've had to consider to also exist in this space."

For Flippin, who is based outside of Denver, such experiences are personal. She was on the receiving end of anti-Asian verbal harassment at the height of the Covid-19 pandemic.

"Pre-pandemic, safety was definitely a consideration for me out on the trails, but it wasn't at the forefront of my mind like oh this could be a potentially dangerous place for us to be living in or for me," Flippin says. "During that time, I was harassed, stared at and called racial slurs out on the trails, and now I just don't have the same sense of empowerment and autonomy to run by myself on the trails like I used to, especially now that I live in a much more rural area."

FOR MANY TRAIL and ultramarathon aficionados, races like Western States and UTMB are viewed as the holy grail in this corner of the space. But they're also some of the least accessible, as they are notoriously difficult to gain entry. Entry fees are over $400 and $500, respectively, and participants also have to account for significant travel costs, as well as shoes, gear, and other training necessities.

"UTMB week is pitched as like the 'Super Bowl of trail running, or the who's who of trail running,' where amateur athletes can run the same trails as elite athletes. But in order to do that, you have to have a good disposable income, so we need to look at the fallacy around 'if you want to be here, you can,' when it's really more like 'if you look a certain way and if you have a certain means of financial support, you could be here,'" Pace-Humphreys says. "Brands and race directors need to put their money where their mouths are, because what we're seeing geopolitically across the board at the moment is a massive pullback in terms of budgets for any kind of DEI initiatives."

Désir emphasizes the need for tracking racial demographic data at large races, even though being confronted with and potentially publicizing low numbers is likely to make leadership uncomfortable. Bailey echoes that sentiment, noting that trail running brands and community leaders need to be cognizant about who they choose to partner with if they care about making meaningful changes.

Although the trail running community still has huge strides to make to become less homogeneous, there are small signs of progress that will hopefully encourage more runners of color to enter the space. This year, Ben Dhiman, who is Indian-American, won the prestigious 108-mile UTMB race, becoming the first runner to break 19 hours and just the second American man to win. Earlier this year, Jennifer Lichter—who was born in Bogotá, Colombia, and lived there until she was nine years old—set a new women’s course record at the Black Canyon 100K, becoming the first woman to finish in under eight hours. Lichter also broke the women’s course record at Western States, which was also her first 100-mile race.

Outside the pro ranks, the programs and creator-led efforts leading the way are also making their mark to bring more everyday people outside.

"Our experience with the RIDC program showed that the doors of the trails are open for you to come as you are—that you already belong," Haugabook says. "It's allowed us to take up space unapologetically—space that's rightfully already ours, and not the other way around."

After all, expanding the field is the only way true growth happens. Bailey has seen that in the mainstream running world—and he knows that’s the way forward on the trails, too.

"Road running is not booming because it stayed white and pushed white and just kept going the same—more voices from different communities and different countries now affect what we all look like and feel like, and how we dress, and the shoes we're wearing," Bailey says. "All of that has come from more voices pouring into it, whether that's in run clubs or who brands decide will wear their clothes and send to events. Trail leadership needs to also remember that is how things are grown and move forward, and if you want to evolve, and be more welcoming in general, then you have to start investing in inviting people that maybe wouldn't otherwise be there."

Emilia Benton is a Houston-based freelance writer and editor. In addition to Runner's World, she has contributed health, fitness and wellness content to Women's Health, SELF, Prevention, Healthline, and the Houston Chronicle, among other publications. She is also an 11-time marathoner, a USATF Level 1-certified running coach, and an avid traveler.

#704. Oxford scientists discover how viruses build themselves without a blueprint

📅 Oct 22, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Researchers at the University of Oxford have observed virus-like particles assembling themselves one molecule at a time, uncovering the surprisingly simple rules that allow complex biological structures to form with remarkable reliability. The findings, published in Nature, offer a detailed look at a process that scientists have struggled to observe directly for decades.

Understanding how viruses construct their protective protein shells, and how that construction might be interrupted, could lead to new antiviral drugs, improved vaccines, and other medical treatments. The discovery helps explain how individual proteins consistently organize themselves into the correct structures despite having thousands of possible ways to connect.

The researchers found that viral assembly resembles navigating a complicated maze, where only certain paths lead to lasting progress. At first, protein components form weak connections that can easily break apart if the arrangement is unsuccessful. This flexibility allows the molecules to keep trying different combinations until they form particular closed structures. Once multiple connections hold these structures together, they become far more stable.

These stable arrangements serve as molecular checkpoints, narrowing the enormous number of possible assembly routes into a much smaller set of successful pathways. One especially important milestone occurs when five larger protein building blocks connect to form a closed pentagonal ring. This is the first highly stable structure in the assembly process. Once it forms, each subsequent stable stage requires fewer additional building blocks, allowing the particle to grow more quickly.

Co-lead author Dr. Roi Asor (Department of Chemistry and Kavli Institute for Nanoscience Discovery, University of Oxford) explained: "A virus has to solve an extraordinary construction problem. Its components somehow have to find the right arrangement among a huge number of possibilities, without a blueprint or machinery directing the process. We can now watch what happens molecule by molecule and see the physical rules that make it possible."

Watching Virus Particles Assemble Molecule by Molecule

Viruses store and protect their genetic material within organized protein shells known as capsids. Depending on the virus, these shells may consist of tens or even thousands of individual protein components. Remarkably, in many cases, the proteins can arrange themselves into the correct structure without any external machinery directing their assembly.

Although scientists have investigated viral self-assembly for decades, capturing the process as it unfolds has been a major challenge. Many of the intermediate structures exist only briefly and appear in very small quantities. As a result, traditional methods have generally allowed researchers to examine the starting materials and finished particles without clearly showing how one transforms into the other.

To overcome this limitation, the Oxford researchers studied an engineered virus-like particle composed of 60 protein units. They used mass photometry, a technique developed at Oxford that determines the mass of individual molecules by measuring the light they scatter. By combining this technology with a new approach for keeping individual molecules confined and continuously observable, the team could repeatedly measure the mass of a growing particle as additional proteins attached.

This approach allowed the scientists to follow the construction process as it happened, effectively tracking the addition of individual molecular building blocks over time.

Co-first author PhD student Dan Loewenthal (Department of Chemistry and Kavli Institute for Nanoscience Discovery, University of Oxford) said: "This method lets us study the assembly process directly, we just take a video!"

Co-lead author Professor Philipp Kukura (Department of Chemistry and Kavli Institute for Nanoscience Discovery, Oxford University) said: "Until now, much of our understanding of how these structures assemble has had to be reconstructed from snapshots or theoretical models. Being able to both quantify the underlying interactions and follow one particle as it grows changes that. We can see the important intermediate structures appear and deduce a detailed model of the construction process."

The Surprising Trick That Makes Viral Assembly Work

The results help explain how a process involving an overwhelming number of possible molecular arrangements can consistently produce the correct structure. Rather than exploring every possible route, the proteins progress through a relatively small number of stable intermediate stages that guide assembly toward completion.

The findings also clarify why the earliest stage of construction, called nucleation, is particularly difficult and why growth becomes substantially faster after the first stable structure appears.

A key part of this efficiency comes from the ability of protein building blocks to make mistakes without permanently disrupting the process. Weak initial connections allow incorrect combinations to separate, giving the proteins another opportunity to find a better arrangement. When enough components eventually connect in the correct closed configuration, the resulting structure becomes stable enough to support further growth.

Dr. Asor added: "The weak interactions give the system room to make mistakes. Most encounters don't have to be successful: the components can separate and try again. But once enough of them come together in the right closed arrangement, the structure becomes stable, and assembly can move forward. That combination of trial and error followed by locking in successful structures is what makes the process so reliable."

New Possibilities for Antiviral Drugs and Vaccines

By identifying the molecular interactions that control viral assembly, scientists may eventually be able to develop treatments that interfere with the construction of viral protein shells. The same insights could also support efforts to engineer virus-like particles for vaccines and other therapeutic applications.

Co-author Dr. Jack Tan (MRC Weatherall Institute of Molecular Medicine and CAMS-Oxford Institute) noted: "The ability to understand these processes at the molecular level could have important applications in vaccine development and antivirals that disrupt viral assembly."

The implications may extend far beyond viruses. Self-assembly is essential throughout biology, helping create everything from the internal skeletons of cells to specialized molecular compartments and large protein complexes.

By making it possible to observe these intricate construction processes one molecule at a time, the Oxford team's approach offers researchers a powerful new way to investigate how complex biological structures emerge from relatively simple interactions.

Materials provided by University of Oxford. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#4577. Why Living with Endometriosis May Double Your Odds of Migraine

📅 Oct 22, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

In a new analysis, researchers found females with endometriosis were 2.3 times more likely to also have migraine, and they said the two conditions may share a pain pathway involving nerves and inflammation.Recognizing that connection earlier could lead to coordinated care for both conditions, the researchers said.The analysis, presented at the American Neurological Association (ANA) Annual Meeting in San Diego on October 20, included more than 13 million females. The research hasn’t yet been published in a peer-reviewed scientific journal.

Endometriosis occurs when tissue similar to what lines the uterus grows outside the uterus, Mallory Stuparich, MD, a gynecologic surgeon at MemorialCare Miller Children’s & Women’s Hospital in Long Beach, California, told Healthline. Stuparich was not involved in the study.Yohannes Woubishet Woldeamanuel, MD, lead author of the study and assistant professor of neurology at Mayo Clinic Arizona, told Healthline that migraine also involves more than one part of the body.“Migraine is more than a headache,” he said.Headache is one symptom, Woldeamanuel said, but migraine can also cause sensory, motor, and balance symptoms.“Migraine is a little complex. It’s a multiple-nervous system involving a condition,” he said.

Stuparich said endometriosis pain typically develops in 2 ways. The first starts in the lesions themselves.“There are inflammatory markers that are created by the endometriosis lesions themselves, and those inflammatory markers can irritate the nerves that live close by the lesions,” she said.The second involves the nervous system, Stuparich said. Scar tissue can grow its own nerve supply, and years of chronic pain can change how the nervous system responds. That adaptation can “perpetuate pain or make people feel sensations that normally wouldn’t be painful,” she said.Stuparich said the inflammatory and immune processes involved in endometriosis have “pretty significant overlap in the setting of migraines.”

One possible link between the two conditions is calcitonin gene-related peptide (CGRP), a chemical messenger that helps transmit pain signals, the researchers said.CGRP plays a key role in migraine attacks, Woldeamanuel said, and blocking it reduces those attacks. CGRP medications work by blocking the sites where CGRP attaches or by binding to CGRP itself.Researchers have also found CGRP in endometriosis tissue.

A 2024 study published in Science Translational Medicine found CGRP in endometriosis lesions from both people and mice. In mice, blocking CGRP signaling reduced pain and reduced lesion size.

Other studies point to shared biology between the two conditions:

Stuparich said she was “not surprised at all” by the finding because it matches what she sees in her own patients.“We see this overlap of patients who have endometriosis also having migraines,” she said.Stuparich said the science still has gaps. She noted that nothing has been proven conclusively in peer-reviewed research yet, but the way both diseases behave supports a connection.“It makes sense that there would be overlap,” she said.

Endometriosis and migraine are treated by different specialists, so the overlap is easy to miss, Woldeamanuel said.For the analysis, researchers at Mayo Clinic Arizona reviewed 35 studies published over 50 years, comparing migraine rates among 207,675 females with endometriosis and 12,963,120 females without the condition.Short-term health records miss up to 90% of that overlap, according to the analysis. Migraine rates in females with endometriosis changed depending on how each study identified migraine.“This data is hiding,” Woldeamanuel said.

“These two clinics are siloed. One clinic doesn’t ask about the other condition. They just focus on their own specialty,” Woldeamanuel said.The findings of the analysis have not been published in a peer-reviewed journal.

Some treatments already overlap, Stuparich said. Hormonal birth control “can sometimes be used to treat menstrually associated migraines,” she said.Stuparich said she has heard anecdotally of patients whose endometriosis symptoms improved after they started CGRP medications for migraine, though long-term data are still lacking.The researchers said the findings support studying whether existing CGRP-blocking migraine medications could also relieve endometriosis pain, potentially offering one treatment for both conditions.Woldeamanuel leads a planned Mayo Clinic trial testing one of those medications, rimegepant (Nurtec ODT), for endometriosis pain. The trial will also track migraine days in participants who have both conditions. It’s expected to finish in 2031, according to ClinicalTrials.gov.

People who suspect they have both conditions can take steps now to get them treated together, Stuparich said.Patients can raise the connection themselves, Stuparich said. She suggested a starting point for each appointment:

Patients can also ask for an early referral to the other specialist so both conditions get coordinated care, Woldeamanuel said. “The earlier it’s diagnosed, the better it’s managed,” he said.Stuparich said doctors are moving away from viewing endometriosis as only a gynecologic disorder because it can affect the whole body.“It is a whole body systemic neuroinflammatory neuroimmune disorder,” she said.

#4379. I keep embarrassing myself in front of the nature gods in handpainted 2D roguelike Talaka, whose colourful, combative demo is out now

📅 Oct 21, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

One of the most memorable demos I played at Gamescom Latam 2026 was that of Talaka, a watercoloured 2D action-roguelike that - like many games at the São Paulo-based show – leaned deeply into Brazilian culture and mythology. In this case, it's something of a side-scrolling Hades, with the permahorny Greek gods replaced by a playful pantheon of Orishas (vaguely human-shaped forces of nature) hailing from the Afro-Brazilian Candomblé religion. Though no fault of theirs, I kinda sucked at Talaka when I played it then, and the demo currently available during Steam Next Fest confirms I kinda suck at it now – though it's still colourful enough, and steeped in a folklore that games so rarely explore, that I'm going to keep an eye on it.

The Hades comparison isn't just me doing some divine typecasting: even with the different perspective, some light platforming, and the fact that Talaka herself is an isekai'd artist instead of a rogue demigod, the structure is the same. Pick a weapon, clear a room of lesser mythological minions, get chatted up by an upgrade-granting deity. Repeat steps 2 and 3 until death.

Talaka (the game) still stands out, though, in part on the vibrancy of its entirely handpainted art. Emphasis on the paint: Talaka's (the person) melee swings and magic blasts are illustrated with thick, black brushstrokes in the weaponry's wake, and in place of any gore, fallen foes leave behind splotches of colour, daubing the backdrop with your victories. Developers Potato Kid have come up with some impressively distinct Orisha designs, too. If I had to pick a favourite, it might be Oshossi, Orisha of hunting and the wilderness, who pairs his draping golden cloths with a fine yet aggressively wide-brimmed feather hat.

It's always nice to play something steeped in a real-life culture that most games ignore - it's a learning experience, as much as anything else. And Talaka also seems careful not to stretch its myths so far that they become original fantasy. With the exception of the more mystical Orisha realm, which serves as a House of Hades/Crossroads-style hub, backdrops are based on real regions of Brazil, keeping things somewhat grounded even as you battle skeletal rattlesnakes and monsterfied versions of 19th century French mercenaries. The soundtrack, too, hops and kicks with the lively, percussive sounds of traditional Afro-Brazilian instruments.

I've had plenty of time to appreciate such presentational touches, as those skelesnakes and their fleshless mates have been sending me back to the start with the reliability of a Swiss ski lift. The demo doesn't give the impression that this is, title case, A Hard Game, and in fact lavishes you with free bonus damage for chaining kills without stopping to heal - a telltale sign that it wants you to muck in. I think I'm just crap at it, though? Possibly by being a bit too eager to play like the Doom Slayer, and not watching for sudden boss attacks. The vast majority of times I've been sent grovelling back to the Orisha den, it was by something with a named health bar.

Not to also blame my tools, but I've maybe had trouble getting a feel for Talaka's movement and combat; it doesn't have the free-flowing immediacy of a Hades or a Dead Cells, with the platforming in particular being on the floaty side. For all the paint-sloshing visual flair of thwacking a baddie, there's also a sense that more could be done to give those strikes a tangible, weighty impact.

It's nothing that can't be polished or adjusted, mind, and the same goes for some of the demo's more cosmetic blemishes – English script typos, mainly. Talaka doesn't have a formal release date tying it down, so there's time for the jumping and fighting to be brought up to the standard of the game's visual and atmospheric artistry. I'm keen to see where it goes.

Rock Paper Shotgun is better when you sign in
