#792. Too much RNA can starve cells of energy, scientists discover

📅 Oct 21, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Scientists have uncovered a surprising way that viruses can interfere with the body's cells. When too much RNA accumulates inside a cell, it can damage the structures that produce energy, potentially leaving the cell struggling to function normally.

A new study led by researchers at the Texas A&M College of Veterinary Medicine and Biomedical Sciences (VMBS) found that excess RNA produced during poxvirus infections can impair mitochondria, the tiny structures responsible for generating most of the energy cells need to survive.

Published in Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences, the research reveals an unexpected connection between RNA buildup and cellular energy production. The findings could have implications far beyond viral infections, potentially helping scientists better understand aging-related diseases and improve RNA-based medical treatments, including mRNA vaccines.

"Scientists have long known that RNA degradation helps control protein production and remove defective RNA," said Dr. Zhilong Yang, a professor in VMBS' Department of Veterinary Pathobiology. "Our study reveals another important role: it helps cells maintain the energy they need to function properly."

Cells constantly produce and break down RNA as part of their normal activities. This cleanup process, known as RNA degradation, helps regulate protein production, eliminate faulty RNA, and control immune responses. The new findings suggest it also performs another essential function by protecting the cell's energy supply.

RNA plays several important roles in keeping cells functioning. Messenger RNA (mRNA), for example, carries genetic instructions that tell cells which proteins to produce. These proteins are needed for nearly everything cells do, from maintaining their structure to carrying out essential chemical reactions.

Another type, double-stranded RNA (dsRNA), frequently appears during viral infections. Its presence can serve as a warning signal, alerting the immune system that a virus may have entered the cell.

Viruses often generate large quantities of RNA as they reproduce inside infected cells. Ordinarily, cellular cleanup systems help prevent RNA from accumulating to harmful levels. However, when RNA production exceeds the cell's ability to break it down, the excess material can interfere with mitochondria and reduce energy production.

Scientists already understood that excessive double-stranded RNA could provoke potentially harmful immune responses. What surprised the researchers was discovering that too much ordinary messenger RNA could also damage mitochondria.

"MRNA is a normal component of our cells, but it seems like with many good things, if we get too much, that can become a problem for the cell," Yang said. "When too much RNA builds up, including mRNA, it can damage the mitochondria and interfere with the cell's ability to produce energy."

This creates an unusual challenge for viruses. Although viruses depend entirely on the cells they infect to supply energy and manufacture proteins, producing excessive amounts of viral RNA can damage the very machinery they need to reproduce.

According to Yang, poxviruses, a group that includes the viruses responsible for smallpox and mpox, appear to have ways of managing this problem. They use RNA cleanup mechanisms to remove excess RNA, helping infected cells remain functional long enough for viral replication to continue.

"If the cell is not healthy, the virus would not replicate well," Yang said. "The virus needs to keep RNA levels balanced inside the cell."

Scientists Discover RNA Can Directly Damage Mitochondria

One of the study's most unexpected findings emerged when researchers investigated exactly how excess RNA affects mitochondria.

Initially, the team suspected that RNA buildup was damaging these cellular powerhouses indirectly by activating the immune system. Certain types of RNA are known to trigger defensive responses, which can place additional stress on infected cells.

However, experiments revealed that the damage did not require an immune response.

"When we isolated only the mitochondria and added RNA, we still saw mitochondrial damage," said Dr. Djamal Brahim Belhaouari, the study's first author. "That was the moment we realized this could be a previously unknown cellular response to RNA."

The researchers also observed that mitochondrial problems developed before substantial immune activation occurred. Furthermore, the damage did not depend on the immune pathways normally activated during infection.

Together, these results indicate that excess RNA itself can interfere with mitochondrial function, rather than simply causing damage through the body's response to a virus.

The precise mechanism remains unclear, but the researchers have proposed a possible explanation involving electrical charges.

Mitochondria produce energy by maintaining an electrical and chemical difference across their inner membranes. This difference helps drive the production of ATP, the molecule cells use as their primary energy source.

RNA carries a negative electrical charge. The researchers suspect that when unusually large amounts accumulate near mitochondria, they may disturb the electrical conditions required for efficient energy production.

"We think the negatively charged RNA may accumulate around mitochondria and disrupt the electrical balance needed for energy production, although how it happens is unknown," Yang said. "This is a new concept that we are continuing to explore."

What the Discovery Could Mean for Aging and Disease

Although the experiments focused on viral infections, the findings raise questions about whether RNA buildup might contribute to other health problems.

Abnormal RNA accumulation has been observed in several conditions, including certain cancers, neurodegenerative diseases, and disorders associated with aging. Neurodegenerative diseases involve the progressive deterioration of nerve cells, as occurs in conditions such as Alzheimer's and Parkinson's disease.

Because mitochondria are essential for maintaining healthy cells, disruptions to their energy production could potentially contribute to cellular dysfunction in some of these conditions.

The researchers suggest that excess RNA may represent one previously unrecognized source of mitochondrial stress. However, further studies are needed to determine how broadly this mechanism operates and whether it directly contributes to these diseases.

"We think this has quite broad translational implications," Brahim Belhaouari said. "Too much double-stranded RNA or mRNA that cannot be degraded effectively may contribute to disease processes in some contexts."

Potential Implications for mRNA Vaccines and Future Treatments

The discovery could also provide useful information for scientists developing medicines that rely on RNA.

RNA-based therapies, including mRNA vaccines, work by introducing RNA instructions into cells to produce specific proteins. Understanding how cells respond to RNA, process it, and eventually break it down is important for designing these treatments.

By identifying another way excessive RNA can affect cellular health, the findings may help researchers optimize future RNA-based therapies and vaccines. The study does not establish that existing mRNA vaccines cause mitochondrial damage in people.

"Understanding this mechanism could help researchers design and optimize future RNA-based therapeutics and vaccines by better understanding how cells respond to large amounts of RNA," Yang said.

More broadly, the research suggests that controlling RNA levels is important for more than maintaining accurate protein production or managing immune activity. It may also be essential for keeping mitochondria functioning and ensuring cells have enough energy.

For the scientists, the most striking result was that even a molecule cells depend on every day can become harmful when it accumulates beyond normal levels.

"Scientists already knew that too much double-stranded RNA can be harmful," Yang said. "What surprised us was finding out that too much mRNA -- something cells normally need every day -- can also damage mitochondria and interfere with the cell's ability to produce energy."

Research reported in this study was supported, in part, by the National Institute of Allergy and Infectious Diseases (R01AI143709 to Z.Y.) and Texas A&M University. The content is solely the responsibility of the authors and does not necessarily represent the official views of the NIH.

#4586. RFK Jr.’s Revised Vaccine Schedule May Cause 83% Surge in Hepatitis B Infections

📅 Oct 21, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

Health and Human Services Secretary Robert F. Kennedy Jr.’s proposed changes to the childhood vaccine schedule have raised alarm among public health experts and researchers.

Concerns include a rise in preventable infectious diseases such as measles, polio, and hepatitis B.

A new study suggests that delaying or foregoing the hepatitis B shot for newborns could increase chronic infections among children.

In December 2025, the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC) ended its recommendation for universal hepatitis B vaccination at birth for infants whose mothers test negative for the virus. Instead, the federal health agency recommends individual-based clinical decision making.

After reviewing the evidence, the CDC, which is overseen by the HHS, concluded that newborns “face little risk of contracting hepatitis,” which deemed the vaccine, typically administered at birth, unnecessary.

Experts say this shift in public health policy wasn’t based on science, and researchers warn of rising infection rates.

“Hepatitis B immunizations have been proven to protect individuals against a serious and significant disease,” said Graham Tse, MD, pediatrician and chief medical officer of MemorialCare Miller Children’s & Women’s Hospital in Long Beach, CA.

“The hepatitis B vaccines have several decades of history and have been proven safe and effective,” Tse said. “There has been no new data or evidence presented or uncovered to support delaying the newborn dose of hepatitis B.”

Here’s what parents should know about making informed decisions with their child’s healthcare team.

A U.S. modeling study published in Annals of Internal Medicine on October 20 projected that delaying the first vaccine dose until 2 months could increase chronic hepatitis B infections among infants by 19% under one scenario.

With shared decision making and lower vaccine uptake, projected increases ranged from 83% to 225%, compared with universal birth-dose vaccination.

The researchers also estimated that up to 1.8 million young adults could remain susceptible to hepatitis B by age 20 under shared decision making, compared with 232,000 under universal birth dosing.

Another study published in JAMA Pediatrics in late April projected hundreds of additional chronic infections annually if the hepatitis B birth dose were delayed or omitted.

However, these figures are model-based projections, rather than verified outcomes. Actual results will depend on vaccination rates and other factors.

In December 2025, the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC) adopted a recommendation shifting away from universal hepatitis B vaccination at birth for infants whose mothers test negative for hepatitis B surface antigen (HBsAg).

Under the new approach, parents and healthcare professionals can decide together whether to give the first dose at birth or defer it until the baby is 2 months old.

The change followed an 8 to 3 vote by the Advisory Committee on Immunization Practices (ACIP), marking a departure from longstanding U.S. guidance.

However, infants born to mothers who test positive for hepatitis B or whose infection status is unknown should still receive the hepatitis B vaccine and hepatitis B immune globulin (HBIG) within 12 hours of birth, according to the updated guidelines.

Hepatitis B is a viral infection that affects the liver.

Although some infections resolve, newborns and infants face a higher risk of developing lifelong hepatitis B infection compared with adults.

Up to 90% of infants infected during their first year develop chronic hepatitis B.

“We have actual world evidence that vaccinating newborns for hepatitis B prevents hepatitis B infections later in life and the serious complications of that disease,” Tse said.

Over time, chronic infection can lead to cirrhosis, liver failure, or liver cancer.

“The danger of delaying the newborn hepatitis B shot is that individuals do not get vaccinated and thereby do not have protection against hepatitis B,” Tse said.

Early vaccination helps protect young infants before potential exposure occurs, including situations in which maternal screening misses an infection.

“Newborns can be infected with hepatitis B from a mother who is not aware they have a hepatitis B infection or from exposure later in life,” Tse said.

“The risk is developing a hepatitis B infection, which can potentially lead to liver cancer, fulminant hepatitis, or severe end-stage liver disease.”

In contrast to the CDC, the American Academy of Pediatrics (AAP) still recommends a universal hepatitis B vaccine dose within 24 hours of birth, followed by additional doses during infancy.

The leading pediatric medical organization has raised concerns about the potential consequences of reduced vaccination rates.

The hepatitis B vaccine has decades of safety and effectiveness data.

Tse said the AAP’s vaccine schedule and recommendations are based on decades of study, use, and expert input, and provide the best protection against vaccine-preventable diseases.

Parents who have questions about potential side effects, timing, or their baby’s individual risk should discuss them with a qualified healthcare professional.

“Parents are confused, given the recommendations from CDC, HHS, and Kennedy differ from established and respected groups such as the American Academy of Pediatrics, the Infectious Disease Society of America, and several states,” Tse said.

“Parents desire to keep their children safe, and having differing opinions and confusion will lead to reduced vaccination rates as parents experience doubt as to whom to listen to or believe.”

While the updated CDC policy offers more flexibility for some families, lower vaccination uptake could lead to an increase in chronic hepatitis B infections.

Ask your pediatrician about the benefits of vaccination against hepatitis B at birth, the risks of delaying the first dose, and the recommended schedule for completing the series.

You can also ask how maternal screening results affect your baby’s protection.

An informed conversation with your healthcare professional can help you weigh the evidence and choose a vaccination plan for your baby.

“I see no argument whatsoever for changing the established vaccination schedule for hepatitis B,” Tse said.

“There have been no new studies, no data, nor evidence to justify Kennedy’s proposed changes to the childhood immunization schedule. Delaying vaccinations or skipping vaccinations puts your child at risk for serious and significant diseases, which are all preventable,” Tse said.

#5239. Just 4 minutes a day can dramatically improve strength after 65

📅 Oct 21, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

A daily strength training routine lasting just four minutes can significantly improve strength, balance, and mobility in older adults, according to new research led by Penn State College of Medicine. The findings suggest that maintaining physical independence with age may require much less exercise than many people assume.

Published in the journal PLOS One, the study found that adults aged 65 and older experienced meaningful improvements in physical performance after only 12 weeks. These gains are especially important because muscle strength and mobility influence the risk of falling, the ability to live independently, and overall health and longevity.

For older adults, being able to move comfortably and perform everyday activities is essential for maintaining quality of life. Simple tasks such as standing up from a chair, climbing stairs, and walking can become more difficult as strength and balance decline.

According to the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention, unintentional injuries, including those caused by falls, are among the leading causes of death in adults aged 65 and older.

Despite the importance of physical fitness, many older adults believe that meaningful improvements require long, demanding workouts. Christopher Sciamanna, professor of medicine and public health at Penn State College of Medicine and lead author of the study, says the new findings challenge that assumption. Even a brief daily routine can improve important measures of mobility and physical function.

"The human body is designed to improve very quickly," Sciamanna said. "And just a few repetitions of an exercise performed regularly can lead to huge improvements. Exercise is about forward thinking -- think about what you want to be able to do and train for it."

Why Shorter Workouts Could Make Exercise Easier

Strength training, also known as resistance training, involves making muscles work against some form of resistance, such as body weight or elastic bands. Research has shown that it can substantially improve muscle strength within a few months.

However, fewer than one in five older adults meet the recommendation to perform muscle strengthening activities at least twice a week. Common obstacles include discomfort, limited time, and the physical demands of conventional exercise programs.

Another challenge is that planning an effective workout can feel overwhelming, particularly for people who are unfamiliar with exercise.

"Exercise is actually really complicated, because you have to decide how many repetitions, how far, how many sets, how much rest, and how many times per week," said co-author Smita Dandekar, associate professor of pediatrics at Penn State College of Medicine. "It's hard work, so there's huge problems with people wanting to do exercise. If we can make it short, we're part way there."

The researchers had already seen encouraging results from an earlier experiment called FAST (Functional Activity Strength Training)-1. In that smaller study, 24 older adults performed just 30 seconds of push-ups and squats each day. After six months, their ability to perform squats had improved.

Other research has similarly suggested that performing only a few sets of exercises each week can produce strength improvements approaching those achieved with considerably longer workouts.

These findings led Sciamanna and his colleagues to investigate whether an especially brief exercise program could produce measurable improvements in older adults.

Testing a Simple Four-Minute Exercise Routine

For the new study, the Penn State researchers developed a program called FAST-2 to evaluate whether a short daily workout could improve mobility and physical ability in adults older than 65.

The trial included 97 participants whose average age was 74. They were randomly divided into two groups, with one following the exercise program and the other receiving no intervention.

Before the study began, participants reported exercising for an average of only about 18 minutes per week. That was far below the general recommendation of at least 150 minutes of moderate physical activity or 75 minutes of vigorous activity each week, Sciamanna explained.

The FAST-2 routine consisted of four simple movements:

Each exercise lasted 30 seconds, followed by 30 seconds of rest, bringing the complete routine to four minutes.

Participants received four elastic resistance bands and an adjustable-height stepper, along with written instructions explaining how to perform each movement. The researchers also provided easier variations to accommodate different physical abilities.

For example, participants could perform push-ups against a wall or countertop rather than on the floor. Those who found chair stands challenging could use their hands on their knees for additional support.

As their strength improved, participants were encouraged to make the exercises progressively more difficult. Someone performing modified push-ups might advance to the standard movement, while another participant might increase the height of the stepper.

To evaluate the program, researchers measured how quickly and effectively participants could rise from a chair and how long they could balance on one leg. These assessments were conducted at the beginning, midpoint, and end of the 12-week study.

Such tests are valuable because they reflect movements people rely on throughout the day. Performance on these assessments can also help predict future mobility problems, fall risk, and the potential need for additional care.

Significant Strength and Balance Improvements in Just 12 Weeks

The results showed that the brief exercise program produced significant gains in physical function, even though it involved only 60 seconds of lower body resistance training per session.

Participants demonstrated several measurable improvements:

These changes may seem modest, but they reflect physical abilities that are essential for everyday independence. Stronger legs and better balance can make activities such as getting out of a chair, climbing stairs, and walking easier to perform.

Sciamanna emphasized that these simple physical tests can reveal important information about a person's future health and independence.

"These indicators predict your future ability to go into a nursing home, your future likelihood of falling and of developing difficulty walking," Sciamanna said. "They give you a sense of whether or not you're going to be able to be active in the future."

Although the study measured improvements in physical performance rather than directly testing whether participants experienced fewer falls or avoided nursing home care, the findings suggest that short workouts could help preserve abilities closely tied to independent living.

A Workout Older Adults Can Actually Stick With

Beyond the improvements in strength and mobility, the researchers identified another major advantage of the four-minute routine: participants were able to follow it consistently.

Throughout the study, they completed their exercises on 81% of days.

That level of participation suggests that a short, straightforward workout may be easier to incorporate into daily life than a longer exercise program. It could also help people overcome obstacles such as limited time, discomfort, or difficulty maintaining a regular fitness routine.

For older adults who are largely inactive, even a small amount of consistent resistance training could therefore provide a practical starting point for improving physical function.

The researchers say their results reinforce the idea that exercise sessions do not need to be lengthy to produce meaningful benefits. By focusing on movements that support everyday activities, a few minutes of daily effort may help older adults maintain strength, mobility, and independence.

For Sciamanna, the ultimate purpose of exercise extends beyond simply becoming physically stronger. It is about preserving the ability to participate in life and continue doing the things that matter most.

"Exercise is the key to freedom," he said. "Freedom is the ability to be able to do what you want to do, and I would say that if you can't do what you want to do, you're not free. I approach exercise by thinking of what I want to be able to do in 20 years, and then I train to do that."

Additional Penn State researchers involved in the study included Smita Dandekar of the Department of Pediatrics, Division of Hematology and Oncology at Penn State Health Children's Hospital; Jordan Kurth, Liza S. Rovniak, Noel Ballentine, and Natalia Pierwola-Gawin of the Department of Medicine, Division of General Internal Medicine at Penn State College of Medicine; and Yimeng Shang and Shouhao Zhou of the Department of Public Health Sciences, Division of Biostatistics and Bioinformatics at Penn State College of Medicine.

Jonathan G. Stine also contributed to the research. He is affiliated with the Department of Medicine, Division of Gastroenterology and Hepatology at Penn State Health Milton S. Hershey Medical Center and holds an additional appointment in the Department of Public Health Sciences at Penn State College of Medicine.

Other contributors included Matthew Silvis of the Departments of Orthopedics and Rehabilitation and Family and Community Medicine at Penn State Health Milton S. Hershey Medical Center; Matthew A. Ladwig of Purdue University Northwest; David E. Conroy of the University of Michigan; Kathryn H. Schmitz of the University of Pittsburgh School of Medicine; and Margaret Danilovich of Northwestern University Feinberg School of Medicine.
